# Hawaii

This article is about the U.S. state. For the country, see [Hawaiian Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Kingdom). For the archipelago, see [Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Islands). For the largest island in the archipelago, see [Hawaiʻi (island)](70962.md). For other uses, see [Hawaii (disambiguation)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_\(disambiguation\)).

Not to be confused with [Hawai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawai_\(disambiguation\)) or [Kawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kawaii).

**Hawaii** ([/həˈwaɪ.i/](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/English) [//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/e/e4/En-us-Hawaii.ogg/En-us-Hawaii.ogg.mp3](https://upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/transcoded/e/e4/En-us-Hawaii.ogg/En-us-Hawaii.ogg.mp3)<sup>ⓘ</sup> [*hə-WY-ee*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:Pronunciation_respelling_key);[^cite_note-14] [Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language): *Hawaiʻi* [\[həˈvɐjʔi, həˈwɐjʔi\]](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Help\:IPA/Hawaiian)) is an island [state](18618239.md) of the United States, in the [Pacific Ocean](23070.md) about 2,000 miles (3,200 km) southwest of [the U.S. mainland](83759.md). One of the two [non-contiguous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-contiguous_United_States) U.S. states (along with [Alaska](624.md)), it is the only state not on the North American mainland, the only state that is an [archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago), the only state south of the [Tropic of Cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Cancer), one of only two states, along with [Florida](18933066.md), with regions that have a [tropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_climate), and one of the two U.S. states, along with [Texas](29810.md), that were internationally recognized sovereign countries before becoming U.S. states.[^cite_note-15]

Hawaii consists of 137 [volcanic islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Volcanic_island) that make up almost the entire [Hawaiian archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_archipelago) (the exception is [Midway Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midway_Atoll)). Spanning 1,500 miles (2,400 km), the state is [physiographically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Physical_geography) and [ethnologically](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnology) part of the [Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesia) subregion of [Oceania](22621.md).[^cite_note-16] Hawaii's ocean [coastline](5236.md) is consequently the [fourth-longest in the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_coastline), at about 750 miles (1,210 km).[^cite_note-17] The eight main islands, from northwest to southeast, are [Niʻihau](39688.md), [Kauaʻi](70961.md), [Oʻahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O%CA%BBahu), [Molokaʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moloka%CA%BBi), [Lānaʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C4%81na%CA%BBi), [Kahoʻolawe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaho%CA%BBolawe), [Maui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maui), and [Hawaiʻi](70962.md), after which the state is named; the last is often called the "Big Island" or "Hawaiʻi Island" to avoid confusion with the state or archipelago. The uninhabited [Northwestern Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Hawaiian_Islands) make up most of the [Papahānaumokuākea Marine National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papah%C4%81naumoku%C4%81kea_Marine_National_Monument), the [largest protected area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_national_monuments_of_the_United_States) in the U.S. and the [fourth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_protected_areas) in the world.

Of the [50 U.S. states](12610470.md), Hawaii is the [eighth-smallest in land area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_and_territories_by_area) and the [11th-least populous](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_and_territories_of_the_United_States_by_population); but with 1.4 million residents, it ranks [13th in population density](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_states_and_territories_of_the_United_States_by_population_density). Two-thirds of Hawaii residents live on Oʻahu, home to the state's capital and largest city, [Honolulu](13887.md). Hawaii is one of the most demographically diverse U.S. states, owing to its location in the central Pacific and over two centuries of migration. As one of nine [majority-minority states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_minority_in_the_United_States), it has the only Asian American plurality, the largest [Buddhist community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buddhism_in_the_United_States),[^cite_note-18] and largest proportion of [multiracial people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Multiracial_people) in the U.S.[^cite_note-19] Consequently, Hawaii is a unique [melting pot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melting_pot) of North American and [East Asian](19605700.md) cultures, in addition to its [indigenous Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiians) heritage.

Settled by [Polynesians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesians) sometime between 1000 and 1200 CE, Hawaii was home to numerous independent [chiefdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiefdom).[^cite_note-\:2-20] In 1778, British explorer [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) was the first known non-Polynesian to arrive at the archipelago; early British influence is reflected in the [state flag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Hawaii), which bears a [Union Jack](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Jack). An influx of European and American explorers, traders, and whalers soon arrived, leading to the decimation of the once-isolated indigenous community through the introduction of diseases such as [syphilis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syphilis), [tuberculosis](30653.md), [smallpox](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Smallpox), and [measles](58911.md); the native Hawaiian population declined from between 300,000 and one million to less than 40,000 by 1890.[^cite_note-OHA-21][^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] Hawaii became a unified, internationally recognized [kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Kingdom) in 1810, remaining independent until American and European businessmen [overthrew the monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_the_Hawaiian_Kingdom) in 1893; this led to [annexation by the U.S.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newlands_Resolution) in 1898. As a strategically valuable [U.S. territory](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Hawaii), Hawaii was [attacked by Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor) on December 7, 1941, which brought it global and historical significance, and contributed to America's entry into World War II. Hawaii is the most recent state to [join the union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Admission_Act), on August 21, 1959.[^cite_note-24] In 1993, the U.S. government [formally apologized](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apology_Resolution) for its role in the overthrow of Hawaii's government, which had spurred the [Hawaiian sovereignty movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_sovereignty_movement) and has led to ongoing efforts to obtain redress for the indigenous population.

&#x20;From the late 19th century until the mid-20th century, Hawaii's economy was dominated by a [plantation economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_economy), it remains a major agricultural exporter due to its fertile soil and uniquely tropical climate in the U.S. Its economy has gradually diversified since the mid-20th century, with tourism and military defense becoming the two largest sectors. The state attracts visitors, surfers, and scientists with its diverse natural scenery, tropical climate, abundant public beaches, oceanic surroundings, active volcanoes, and clear skies. However, since the onset of the 21st century, [climate change](5042951.md) has wrought existential threats upon Hawaii and its tourism economy, featuring increasing [drought](37335.md) and water shortages, [sea level rise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sea_level_rise) and hotel flooding in Downtown Honolulu, and [wildfire](56106.md) threats. These factors are compounded by an aging and declining population, with an exodus emigrating to the [U.S. mainland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._mainland). Hawaii hosts the [U.S. Pacific Fleet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._Pacific_Fleet), the world's largest naval command, as well as 75,000 employees of the Defense Department.[^cite_note-\:1-25] Hawaii's isolation results in one of the highest [costs of living](522934.md) in the U.S. However, Hawaii is the third-wealthiest state,[^cite_note-\:1-25] and residents have the longest life expectancy of any U.S. state, at 80.7 years.[^cite_note-26]

## Etymology

The State of Hawaii derives its name from the name of its largest island, [Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_\(island\)). A common explanation of the name of Hawaiʻi is that it was named for [Hawaiʻiloa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiiloa), a figure from Hawaiian oral tradition. He is said to have discovered the islands when they were first settled.[^cite_note-27][^cite_note-28]

The [Hawaiian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language) word *Hawaiʻi* is very similar to [Proto-Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Polynesian_language) *\*Sawaiki*, with the [reconstructed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_reconstruction) meaning 'homeland'.[^cite_note-30] [Cognates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cognate) of *Hawaiʻi* are found in other Polynesian languages, including [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language) (*[Hawaiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiki)*), [Rarotongan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Islands_Maori) (*ʻAvaiki*) and [Samoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoan_language) (*[Savaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Savai'i)*). According to linguists Pukui and Elbert,[^cite_note-31] "elsewhere in Polynesia, *Hawaiʻi* or a cognate is the name of the underworld or of the ancestral home, but in Hawaii, the name has no meaning".[^cite_note-32]

### Spelling of state name

In 1978, Hawaiian was added to the Constitution of the State of Hawaii as an official state language alongside English.[^cite_note-33] The title of the state constitution is *The Constitution of the State of Hawaii*. Article XV, Section 1 of the Constitution uses *The State of Hawaii*.[^cite_note-34] [Diacritics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diacritic) were not used because the document, drafted in 1949,[^cite_note-35] predates the use of the *[ʻokina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBokina)* ⟨ʻ⟩ and the *[kahakō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macron_\(diacritic\))* in modern Hawaiian orthography. The exact spelling of the state's name in the Hawaiian language is *Hawaiʻi*.[^cite_note-36]

In the [Hawaii Admission Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Admission_Act) that granted Hawaiian statehood, the federal government used *Hawaii* as the state name, but most official Hawaiian state government publications, departments, and office titles use *Hawaiʻi*, including the [Governor of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governor_of_Hawai%CA%BBi),[^cite_note-37] the [Hawaiʻi State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_State_Legislature),[^cite_note-38] the [Hawaiʻi State Judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_State_Judiciary),[^cite_note-39] the [University of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi),[^cite_note-40] the [Hawaiʻi State Seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seal_of_Hawaii),[^cite_note-41] the [Flag of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flag_of_Hawai%CA%BBi),[^cite_note-42] and the Hawaiʻi Board on Geographic Names.[^cite_note-43] The Hawaiʻi Tourism Authority's official policy is to "recognize the importance of using these markings to preserve the indigenous language and culture of Hawaiʻi and use them in all forms of communications."[^cite_note-tourism-44]

Map of the Hawaiian islands (USCG)

map of Hawaiian Islands

There are eight main Hawaiian islands. Seven are inhabited, but only six are open to tourists and locals. Niʻihau is privately managed by brothers Bruce and [Keith Robinson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keith_Robinson_\(environmentalist\)); access is restricted to those who have their permission. This island is also home to native Hawaiians. Access to uninhabited [Kahoʻolawe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaho%CA%BBolawe) island is also restricted and anyone who enters without permission is subject to arrest. Kahoʻolawe may also be dangerous since it was a military base during the world wars and could still have unexploded ordnance.

### Topography

Map of the Hawaiian islands

[Bathymetric](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bathymetric) map of main Hawaiian Islands

The Hawaiian [archipelago](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago) is 2,000 mi (3,200 km) southwest of the contiguous United States.[^cite_note-54] Hawaii is the southernmost U.S. state and the second farthest west, after [Alaska](624.md). Like Alaska, Hawaii borders no other U.S. state. It is the only U.S. state not in North America, as it is in [Oceania](22621.md), and the only one completely surrounded by water and entirely an archipelago.

In addition to the eight main islands, the state has many smaller islands and islets. [Kaʻula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka%CA%BBula) is a small island near Niʻihau. The [Northwestern Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Hawaiian_Islands) is a group of nine small, older islands northwest of Kauaʻi that extends from [Nīhoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%ABhoa) to [Kure Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kure_Atoll); they are remnants of much larger volcanic mountains. Across the archipelago are around 130 small rocks and islets, such as [Molokini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molokini), which are made up of either volcanic or marine [sedimentary rock](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sedimentary_rock).[^cite_note-55]

Hawaiʻi's tallest mountain, [Mauna Kea](267366.md), is 13,796 ft (4,205 m) above mean sea level;[^cite_note-56] but it is taller than [Mount Everest](42179.md) when measured from the base of the mountain, which is on the floor of the Pacific Ocean, rising about 33,500 feet (10,200 m).[^cite_note-BBC-57][^cite_note-NOAA-58]

### Geology

See also: [Hawaii hotspot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_hotspot)



*Pāhoehoe* (smooth lava) spills into the ocean, forming new rock

The Hawaiian islands were formed by volcanic activity initiated at an undersea [magma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magma) source called the [Hawaiʻi hotspot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_hotspot). The process continues to build islands; the [tectonic plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tectonic_plate) beneath much of the Pacific Ocean continually moves northwest and the hotspot remains stationary, slowly creating new volcanoes. Because of the hotspot's location, all active land volcanoes are on the southern half of Hawaiʻi Island. The newest volcano, [Kamaʻehuakanaloa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kama%CA%BBehuakanaloa_Seamount) (formerly Lōʻihi), is south of the coast of Hawaiʻi Island.

The last volcanic eruption outside Hawaiʻi Island occurred at [Haleakalā](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haleakal%C4%81) on Maui before the late 18th century, possibly hundreds of years earlier.[^cite_note-59] In 1790, [Kīlauea exploded](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Keanakakoi_eruption); it is the deadliest eruption known to have occurred in the modern era in what is now the United States.[^cite_note-60] Up to 5,405 warriors and their families marching on [Kīlauea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%ABlauea) were killed by the eruption.[^cite_note-61] Volcanic activity and subsequent erosion have created impressive geological features. Hawaii Island has the [second-highest point](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_by_highest_point) among the world's islands.[^cite_note-62]

On the volcanoes' flanks, slope instability has generated damaging earthquakes and related [tsunamis](31161.md), particularly in [1868](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1868_Hawaii_earthquake) and [1975](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1975_Hawaii_earthquake).[^cite_note-PTWC-63] Catastrophic [debris avalanches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Debris_avalanche) on the ocean island volcanoes' submerged flanks have created steep cliffs.[^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65]

[Kīlauea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%ABlauea) erupted in May 2018, opening 22 fissure vents on its eastern [rift zone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rift_zone). The [Leilani Estates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leilani_Estates) and Lanipuna Gardens are within this territory. The eruption destroyed at least 36 buildings and this, coupled with the [lava](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lava) flows and the [sulfur dioxide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sulfur_dioxide) fumes, necessitated the evacuation of more than 2,000 inhabitants from their neighborhoods.[^cite_note-66]

### Flora and fauna

See also: [Endemism in the Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemism_in_the_Hawaiian_Islands) and [List of invasive plant species in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_invasive_plant_species_in_Hawaii)

&#x20;A Hawaiian monk seal rests at French Frigate Shoals.

A [Hawaiian monk seal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_monk_seal) seen at [French Frigate Shoals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_Frigate_Shoals), located in the [Northwestern Hawaiian Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northwestern_Hawaiian_Islands), protected as part of the [Papahānaumokuākea Marine National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papah%C4%81naumoku%C4%81kea_Marine_National_Monument).

The islands of Hawaiʻi are distant from other land habitats, and life is thought to have arrived there by wind, waves (i.e., ocean currents), and wings (i.e., birds, insects, and seeds they may have carried on their feathers). Hawaiʻi has more endangered species and has lost a higher percentage of its endemic species than any other U.S. state.[^cite_note-67] The endemic plant *[Brighamia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brighamia)* now requires hand pollination because its natural pollinator is presumed to be extinct.[^cite_note-68] The two species of *Brighamia*—*B. rockii* and *B. insignis*—are represented in the wild by around 120 individual plants. To ensure that these plants set seed, biologists rappel down 3,000-foot (910 m) cliffs to brush pollen onto their stigmas.[^cite_note-69]

32 extant birds are endemic to the state such as [Laysan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laysan_duck) and [Hawaiian duck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_duck), [nēnē](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nene_\(bird\)), [Hawaiian coot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_coot), [Hawaiian hawk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_hawk), [ʻŌmaʻo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BB%C5%8Cma%CA%BBo), [ʻAkikiki](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBAkikiki), [Palila](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palila), [ʻApapane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBApapane), [ʻIʻiwi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBI%CA%BBiwi), [alawī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawai%CA%BBi_creeper), and [Hawaiian petrel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_petrel).[^cite_note-70]

### Terrestrial ecology

The archipelago's extant main islands have been above the ocean's surface for less than 10 million years, a fraction of the time [biological colonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biological_colonization) and evolution have occurred there. The islands are known for the [environmental diversity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Biodiversity) that occurs on high mountains within a trade winds field. Native Hawaiians developed complex horticultural practices to utilize the surrounding ecosystem for agriculture. Cultural practices developed to enshrine values of environmental stewardship and reciprocity with the natural world, resulting in widespread biodiversity and intricate social and environmental relationships that still persist.[^cite_note-LaDuke_1999-71] On a single island, the climate around the coasts can range from dry tropical (less than 20 inches or 510 millimeters annual rainfall) to wet tropical; on the slopes, environments range from [tropical rainforest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_rainforest) (more than 200 inches or 5,100 millimeters per year), through a [temperate climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Temperate_climate), to [alpine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain) conditions with a cold, dry climate. The rainy climate affects [soil development](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Soil_mechanics), which largely determines ground permeability and the distribution of streams and [wetlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wetlands).[^cite_note-72][^cite_note-73][^cite_note-74]

### Protected areas



[Nā Pali Coast State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N%C4%81_Pali_Coast_State_Park), Kauaʻi

Several areas in Hawaii are under the [National Park Service](161535.md)'s protection.[^cite_note-75] Hawaii has two national parks: [Haleakalā National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haleakal%C4%81_National_Park), near [Kula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kula,_Hawaii) on Maui, which features the dormant volcano Haleakalā that formed east Maui; and [Hawaii Volcanoes National Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Volcanoes_National_Park), in the southeast region of Hawaiʻi Island, which includes the active volcano Kīlauea and its rift zones.

There are three [national historical parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_historical_park): [Kalaupapa National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalaupapa_National_Historical_Park) in Kalaupapa, Molokaʻi, the site of a former leper colony; [Kaloko-Honokōhau National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaloko-Honokohau_National_Historical_Park) in [Kailua-Kona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kailua,_Hawaii_County,_Hawaii) on Hawaiʻi Island; and [Puʻuhonua o Hōnaunau National Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pu%CA%BBuhonua_o_H%C5%8Dnaunau_National_Historical_Park), an ancient place of refuge on Hawaiʻi Island's west coast. Other areas under the National Park Service's control include [Ala Kahakai National Historic Trail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ala_Kahakai_National_Historic_Trail) on Hawaiʻi Island and the [USS *Arizona* Memorial](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Arizona_Memorial) at [Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl_Harbor) on Oʻahu.

President [George W. Bush](3414021.md) proclaimed the [Papahānaumokuākea Marine National Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Papah%C4%81naumoku%C4%81kea_Marine_National_Monument) on June 15, 2006. The monument covers roughly 140,000 square miles (360,000 km<sup>2</sup>) of reefs, atolls, and shallow and deep sea out to 50 miles (80 km) offshore in the Pacific Ocean—an area larger than all the national parks in the U.S. combined.[^cite_note-76]

### Climate

See also: [List of Hawaii tornadoes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaii_tornadoes), [List of Hawaii hurricanes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaii_hurricanes), and [Climate of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Climate_of_Hawaii)



Partly [cloudy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cloud_cover) conditions and a gentle breeze at 1\:43 PM [HDT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii%E2%80%93Aleutian_Time_Zone);

[^cite_note-77]

&#x20;north is oriented towards the lower right in this photo taken from the [International Space Station](15043.md) on June 24, 2022

Hawaiʻi has a [tropical climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropical_climate). Temperatures and humidity tend to be less extreme because of near-constant [trade winds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trade_winds) from the east. Summer highs reach around 88 °F (31 °C) during the day, with lows of 75 °F (24 °C) at night. Winter day temperatures are usually around 83 °F (28 °C); at low elevation they seldom dip below 65 °F (18 °C) at night. Snow, not usually associated with the tropics, falls at 13,800 feet (4,200 m) on Mauna Kea and [Mauna Loa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauna_Loa) on Hawaii Island in the winter. Snow rarely falls on Haleakalā. [Mount Waiʻaleʻale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mount_Wai%CA%BBale%CA%BBale) on Kauaʻi has the second-highest average annual rainfall on Earth, about 460 inches (12,000 mm) per year. Most of Hawaii experiences only two seasons; the dry season runs from May to October and the wet season is from October to April.[^cite_note-78]

With [climate change](5042951.md), Hawaiʻi is getting [drier](37335.md) and [hotter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_warming).[^cite_note-HawaiiGettingDrier-79][^cite_note-HawaiiGettingHotter-80] The highest temperature recorded in the state, in [Pahala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pahala) on April 27, 1931, is 100 °F (38 °C), tied with [Alaska](624.md) as the lowest record high temperature observed in a U.S. state.[^cite_note-\:0-81] Hawaiʻi's record low temperature is 12 °F (−11 °C), observed in May 1979 on the summit of [Mauna Kea](267366.md). Hawaiʻi is the only state to have never recorded subzero Fahrenheit temperatures.[^cite_note-\:0-81]

Climates vary considerably on each island; they can be divided into [windward and leeward](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Windward_and_leeward) (*koʻolau* and *kona*, respectively) areas based upon location relative to the higher mountains. Windward sides face cloud cover.[^cite_note-82]

### Environmental issues

Hawaii has a decades-long history of hosting more military space for the United States than any other territory or state.[^cite_note-Takumi_1994_8–9-83] This military activity has taken a sharp toll on the environmental health of the Hawaiian archipelago, degrading its beaches and soil and making some places entirely unsafe due to [unexploded ordnance](406804.md).[^cite_note-84] According to scholar [Winona LaDuke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winona_LaDuke): "The vast militarization of Hawaii has profoundly damaged the land. According to the Environmental Protection Agency, there are more federal hazardous waste sites in Hawaii—31—than in any other U.S. state."[^cite_note-85] Hawaii State Representative [Roy Takumi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roy_Takumi) writes in "Challenging U.S. Militarism in Hawaiʻi and Okinawa" that these military bases and hazardous waste sites have meant "the confiscation of large tracts of land from native peoples" and quotes the Hawaiian activist George Helm as asking: "What is national defense when what is being destroyed is the very thing the military is entrusted to defend, the sacred land of Hawaiʻi?"[^cite_note-Takumi_1994_8–9-83] Contemporary Indigenous Hawaiians are still protesting the occupation of their homeland and environmental degradation due to increased militarization in the wake of [9/11](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/9/11).[^cite_note-Kauanui_2004_145–150-86]

After the rise of sugarcane plantations in the mid-19th century, island ecology changed dramatically. Plantations require massive quantities of water, and European and American plantation owners transformed the land in order to access it, primarily by building tunnels to divert water from the mountains to the plantations, constructing reservoirs, and digging wells.[^cite_note-MacLennan_2004_37–62-87] These changes have made lasting impacts on the land and continue to contribute to resource scarcity for Native Hawaiians.[^cite_note-MacLennan_2004_37–62-87][^cite_note-Huard-88]

According to Stanford scientist and scholar Sibyl Diver, Indigenous Hawaiians engage in a reciprocal relationship with the land, "based on principles of mutual caretaking, reciprocity and sharing".[^cite_note-Diver_2019_400–429-89] This relationship ensures the longevity, sustainability, and natural cycles of growth and decay, as well as cultivating a sense of respect for the land and humility towards one's place in an ecosystem.[^cite_note-Diver_2019_400–429-89]

The tourism industry's ongoing expansion and its pressure on local systems of ecology, cultural tradition and infrastructure creates a conflict between economic and environmental health.[^cite_note-90] In 2020, the Center for Biological Diversity reported on the plastic pollution of Hawaii's Kamilo beach, citing "massive piles of plastic waste".[^cite_note-91] [Invasive species](67554.md) are spreading, and chemical and pathogenic runoff is contaminating groundwater and coastal waters.[^cite_note-92]

In June 2022, a lawsuit was filed against the Hawaii Department of Transportation claiming that the state's pro-fossil fuel transportation policies violated their state constitutional rights and created "untenable levels of greenhouse gas emissions." The lawsuit, *Navahine F v. Hawaii Department of Transportation*, was filed by 13 young people in the state, most of whom are Indigenous. The resulting legal settlement will force the state to move more aggressively towards a zero-emission transportation system by 2045.[^cite_note-93]

| [v](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template\:History_of_Hawaii_sidebar)[t](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Template_talk\:History_of_Hawaii_sidebar)[e](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special\:EditPage/Template\:History_of_Hawaii_sidebar) |

Hawaiʻi is one of two U.S. states, along with Texas, that were internationally recognized sovereign nations before becoming U.S. states. The [Kingdom of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hawaii) was sovereign from 1810 until 1893, when resident American and European capitalists and landholders [overthrew the monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii). Hawaiʻi was an independent republic from 1894 until August 12, 1898, when it officially became a U.S. territory. Hawaiʻi was admitted as a U.S. state on August 21, 1959.[^cite_note-94]

### Human migration and arrival – Ancient Hawaiʻi (1000–1778)

Main article: [Ancient Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Hawaii)

The date of human arrival and habitation of the Hawaiian Islands is the subject of academic debate.[^cite_note-95] Early archaeological studies suggested that Polynesians from the [Marquesas Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquesas_Islands) or [Society Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Islands) may have arrived as early as the 3rd century CE.[^cite_note-96] Some archaeologists and historians think it was a later wave of immigrants from [Tahiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahiti) around 1100 CE who introduced a new line of high chiefs, the [kapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapu_\(Hawaiian_culture\)) system, the practice of human sacrifice, and the building of *[heiau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heiau)*.[^cite_note-auto-97][^cite_note-98] This later immigration is detailed in [Hawaiian mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_mythology) (*moʻolelo*) about [Paʻao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pa'ao). Other authors say there is no archaeological or linguistic evidence of a later influx of Tahitian settlers and that Paʻao must be regarded as a myth.[^cite_note-auto-97] More recent archaeological studies further suggest that the first settlement of Hawaii was not until around 900–1200 CE.[^cite_note-\:2-20]

The islands' history is marked by a slow, steady growth in population and the size of the [chiefdoms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chiefdoms), which grew to encompass whole islands. Local chiefs, called [aliʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali'i), ruled their settlements, and launched wars to extend their influence and defend their communities from predatory rivals. Ancient Hawaiʻi was a [caste](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Makaainana)-based society, much like that of Hindus in India.[^cite_note-99] Population growth was facilitated by ecological and agricultural practices that combined upland agriculture (*manuka*), ocean fishing (*makai*), [fishponds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancient_Hawaiian_aquaculture) and gardening systems. These systems were upheld by spiritual and religious beliefs, like the *lokahi*, that linked cultural continuity with the health of the natural world.[^cite_note-LaDuke_1999-71] According to Hawaiian scholar [Mililani Trask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mililani_Trask), the *lokahi* symbolizes the "greatest of the traditions, values, and practices of our people ... There are three points in the triangle—the Creator, *Akua*; the peoples of the earth, *Kanaka Maoli*; and the land, the *ʻaina*. These three things all have a reciprocal relationship."[^cite_note-LaDuke_1999-71][^cite_note-People_of_the_seventh_fire-100]

#### Possible European contact before 1778



Manila-Acapulco galleon trade route, showing onward route to Spain

From 1565 to 1815, [Spanish trading ships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manila_galleon), or galleons, crossed the Pacific along a route from [Acapulco](1446.md) in [New Spain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/New_Spain) (modern Mexico) to [Manila](184334.md) in [Spanish Philippines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Captaincy_General_of_the_Philippines). They made this trip once or twice yearly using a route kept secret to protect the Spanish trade monopoly against competing powers.[^cite_note-101] Some sources speculate that sightings of, or interaction with, Hawaii must have occurred.

> “It seems improbable that Spanish mariners could have made several hundred trips … without becoming Hawaii’s first European discoverers.”[^cite_note-Kane-102]

By 1589, [Ortelius](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abraham_Ortelius) was publishing the [Maris Pacifici](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maris_Pacifici) first ever Pacific map featuring an intriguing resemblance of the Hawaiian Islands *"Los Bolcanes"* and *"La Farfania"* in the middle of the Pacific by the [Tropic of Cancer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tropic_of_Cancer).[^cite_note-103]

In 1743, British Commodore [George Anson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/George_Anson,_1st_Baron_Anson) captured a Spanish galleon on which he found a chart of the Pacific that depicted a group of islands at the same latitude as the Hawaiian islands but with a longitude ten degrees to the east. One of these islands is labeled *La Mesa* ('the Table'). This is conjectured to be Hawaii Island since this “agrees very well with the appearance of [Mauna Loa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mauna_Loa) ... when seen at a great distance”.[^cite_note-104]

Many scholars have refuted claims of Spanish knowledge of Hawaii during this period.[^cite_note-105][^cite_note-106][^cite_note-107] However, based on accounts from native Hawaiian [oral tradition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition), Oscar Spate leaves open the possibility of one-way contact and that it is "likely that Spanish castaways reached Hawaii and survived".[^cite_note-108]

### First recorded contact

Drawing of single-masted sailboat with one spinnaker-shaped sail, carrying dozens of men, accompanied by at least four other canoes

*[Tereoboo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalani%CA%BB%C5%8Dpu%CA%BBu), King of Owyhee, bringing presents to [Captain Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook)* by [John Webber](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Webber) (drawn in 1779, published in 1784)

In January 1778, British Captain [James Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook) encountered the Hawaiian Islands serendipitously while crossing the Pacific during his [third voyage of exploration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_voyage_of_James_Cook). This marked the first documented contact by a European explorer with Hawaiʻi.[^cite_note-109] Cook named the archipelago "the Sandwich Islands" in honor of his sponsor, [John Montagu, 4th Earl of Sandwich](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Montagu,_4th_Earl_of_Sandwich). Cook returned to the Hawaiian Islands in 1779 to resupply and overwinter, anchoring in [Kealakakua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kealakekua_Bay) off Hawaii Island for one month. Relations with the local people were peaceful at first, then deteriorated, and [Cook was among those killed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_James_Cook) when violence broke out between the British and local Hawaiians.

After Cook, Hawaii was not visited by any foreign ships for seven years but after 1786 visits became increasingly frequent. At the end of the 18th century, the [maritime fur trade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_fur_trade) developed between the northwest coast of North America and Asia, bringing ships of many nations to the North Pacific Ocean. The Hawaiian islands became established as a convenient source of supplies and destination for overwintering not only for fur traders but also ships engaged in general cross-Pacific commerce.[^cite_note-Kuykendall-110]<sup>: 20–21 </sup>

Historian [Ralph Kuykendall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ralph_Simpson_Kuykendall) has called the impact of these foreign visitors on the hitherto isolated Hawaiian Islands an "invasion" that "little by little overwhelmed the old culture of the islands".[^cite_note-Kuykendall-110]<sup>: 12 </sup> Over the decades after the first contact, the foreign resident population slowly grew; foreigners imported iron tools, manufactured items, and household utensils; they also introduced firearms, alcohol, tobacco, non-native plants, and—inadvertently—arthropods previously unknown to the islands such as mosquitoes and scorpions.[^cite_note-Kuykendall-110]<sup>: 26–28 </sup> Native Hawaiians were vulnerable to Eurasian diseases to which they had less resistance. It is estimated that the native population had declined by half 40 years after Cook's arrival and continued to decline throughout the 19th century.[^cite_note-OHA-21] During the 1850s, measles killed a fifth of Hawaiʻi's people.[^cite_note-111]

### Kingdom of Hawaiʻi

Main article: [Hawaiian Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Kingdom)

#### House of Kamehameha



Evolution of kingdom borders in Hawaii during Kamehameha's conquests

During the 1780s and early 1790s, the Hawaiian Islands were divided among several warring chiefdoms. In 1795, the fighting ended when Kamehameha, then a chief (*aliʻi*) of Hawaii Island, conquered most of the main islands in the archipelago (including Maui and Oʻahu), then founded the [Hawaiian Kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Kingdom) and the [House of Kamehameha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Kamehameha) dynasty. Kauaʻi (with nearby Niʻihau) remained independent until 1810, when it [joined the Hawaiian Kingdom peacefully](70961.md#Cession_to_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii).[^cite_note-Kuykendall-110]<sup>: 29–60 </sup>



Kamehameha I conquered the Hawaiian Islands and established a unified monarchy across the archipelago.

After [Kamehameha II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamehameha_II) inherited the throne in 1819, American Protestant missionaries to Hawaiʻi converted many Hawaiians to Christianity. Missionaries have argued that one function of missionary work was to "civilize" and "purify" perceived heathenism in the New World. This carried into Hawaiʻi.[^cite_note-FLEXNER_2016_307–331-112][^cite_note-nps.gov-113][^cite_note-Medeiros-114][^cite_note-115][^cite_note-116] According to historical archaeologist James L. Flexner, "missionaries provided the moral means to rationalize conquest and wholesale conversion to Christianity".[^cite_note-FLEXNER_2016_307–331-112] But rather than abandon traditional beliefs entirely, most native Hawaiians merged their [Indigenous religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_religion) with Christianity.[^cite_note-FLEXNER_2016_307–331-112][^cite_note-nps.gov-113] Missionaries used their influence to end many traditional practices, including the *[kapu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kapu_\(Hawaiian_culture\))* system, the prevailing legal system before European contact, and *heiau*, or "temples" to religious figures.[^cite_note-FLEXNER_2016_307–331-112][^cite_note-117][^cite_note-118] *Kapu*, which typically translates to "the sacred", refers to social regulations (like gender and class restrictions) based on spiritual beliefs.

Under the missionaries' guidance, laws against gambling, consuming alcohol, dancing the *[hula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hula)*, breaking the Sabbath, and polygamy were enacted.[^cite_note-nps.gov-113] Without the *kapu* system, many temples and priestly statuses were jeopardized, idols were burned, and participation in Christianity increased.[^cite_note-nps.gov-113][^cite_note-Medeiros-114] When [Kamehameha III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamehameha_III) inherited the throne at age 12, his advisors pressured him to merge Christianity with traditional Hawaiian ways. Under the guidance of his *kuhina nui* (his mother and coregent [Elizabeth Kaʻahumanu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ka%CA%BBahumanu)) and British allies, Hawaiʻi turned into a Christian monarchy with the signing of the [1840 Constitution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1840_Constitution_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii).[^cite_note-119][^cite_note-Medeiros-114] [Hiram Bingham I](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiram_Bingham_I), a prominent Protestant missionary, was a trusted adviser to the monarchy during this period. Other missionaries and their descendants became active in commercial and political affairs, leading to conflicts between the monarchy and its restive American subjects.[^cite_note-120] Missionaries from the [Roman Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church) and from [The Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints) were also active in the kingdom, initially converting a minority of the Native Hawaiian population, but later becoming the largest and second-largest religious denominations on the islands, respectively.[^cite_note-121][^cite_note-122][^cite_note-123][^cite_note-124] Missionaries from each major group administered to the leper colony at Kalaupapa on Molokaʻi, which was established in 1866 and operated well into the 20th century. The best known were [Father Damien](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Father_Damien) and [Mother Marianne Cope](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mother_Marianne_Cope), both of whom were canonized in the early 21st century as Roman [Catholic saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Catholic_saints).

The death of the bachelor [King Kamehameha V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamehameha_V)—who did not name an heir—resulted in the popular election of [Lunalilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lunalilo) over [Kalākaua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kal%C4%81kaua). Lunalilo died the next year, also without naming an heir. In 1874, the election was contested within the legislature between Kalākaua and [Emma, Queen Consort of Kamehameha IV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Emma_of_Hawaii). After riots broke out, the U.S. and Britain landed troops on the islands to restore order. The [Legislative Assembly](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legislature_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii) chose [King Kalākaua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kal%C4%81kaua) as monarch by a vote of 39 to 6 on February 12, 1874.[^cite_note-125]

#### 1887 Constitution and overthrow preparations

In 1887, Kalākaua was forced to sign the [1887 Constitution of the Kingdom of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1887_Constitution_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii). Drafted by white businessmen and lawyers, the document stripped the king of much of his authority. It established a property qualification for voting that effectively disenfranchised most Hawaiians and immigrant laborers and favored the wealthier, white elite. Resident whites were allowed to vote but resident Asians were not. As the 1887 Constitution was signed under threat of violence, it is known as the Bayonet Constitution. King Kalākaua, reduced to a figurehead, reigned until his death in 1891. His sister, Queen [Liliʻuokalani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liliuokalani), succeeded him; she was the last monarch of Hawaiʻi.[^cite_note-126]

&#x20;In 1893, Liliʻuokalani announced plans for a new constitution to proclaim herself an absolute monarch. On January 14, 1893, a group of mostly Euro-American business leaders and residents formed the [Committee of Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_of_Safety_\(Hawaii\)) to stage a [coup d'état](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii) against the kingdom and seek annexation by the United States. U.S. Government Minister [John L. Stevens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_L._Stevens), responding to a request from the Committee of Safety, summoned a company of U.S. Marines. The queen's soldiers did not resist. According to historian William Russ, the monarchy was unable to protect itself.[^cite_note-Adam-127] In *Hawaiian Autonomy*, Liliʻuokalani wrote:&#x20;

> If we did not by force resist their final outrage, it was because we could not do so without striking at the military force of the United States. Whatever constraint the executive of this great country may be under to recognize the present government at Honolulu has been forced upon it by no act of ours, but by the unlawful acts of its own agents. Attempts to repudiate those acts are vain.[^cite_note-128][^cite_note-129]

In a message to Sanford B. Dole, Liliʻuokalani wrote:

> Now to avoid any collision of armed forces and perhaps the loss of life, I do under this protest, and impelled by said force, yield my authority until such time as the Government of the United States shall, upon the facts being presented to it, undo the action of its representatives and reinstate me in the authority which I claim as the constitutional sovereign of the Hawaiian Islands.[^cite_note-\:5-130][^cite_note-131]

### Overthrow of 1893 – Republic of Hawaiʻi (1894–1898)

Main articles: [Overthrow of the Kingdom of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii), [Provisional Government of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_Hawaii), and [Republic of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_of_Hawaii)

See also: [List of Hawaiian sovereignty movement groups § Historical – Royalist Organizations (from 1880s)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaiian_sovereignty_movement_groups#Historical_%E2%80%93_Royalist_Organizations_\(from_1880s\))

The treason trials of 1892 brought together the main players in the 1893 overthrow. American Minister John L. Stevens voiced support for Native Hawaiian revolutionaries; William R. Castle, a Committee of Safety member, served as a defense counsel in the treason trials; Alfred Stedman Hartwell, the 1893 annexation commissioner, led the defense effort; and Sanford B. Dole ruled as a supreme court justice against acts of conspiracy and treason.[^cite_note-132]

Queen Liliʻuokalani, seated inside ʻIolani Palace

[Queen Liliʻuokalani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liliuokalani), the last reigning monarch of the Hawaiian Kingdom

On January 17, 1893, a small group of sugar and pineapple-growing businessmen, aided by the U.S. minister to Hawaii and backed by heavily armed U.S. soldiers and marines, deposed Queen Liliʻuokalani and installed a provisional government composed of members of the Committee of Safety.[^cite_note-\:4-133] According to scholar Lydia Kualapai and Hawaii State Representative Roy Takumi, this committee was formed against the will of Indigenous Hawaiian voters, who constituted the majority of voters at the time. According to scholar J Kehaulani Kauanui, it consisted of "thirteen white men".[^cite_note-KUALAPAI_2005_32–62-134][^cite_note-Takumi_1994_8–9-83][^cite_note-Kauanui_2004_145–150-86] Stevens conspired with U.S. citizens to overthrow the monarchy.[^cite_note-HIgpo-135] After the overthrow, Dole, a citizen of Hawaii and cousin to James Dole, owner of Hawaiian Fruit Company, a company that benefited from the annexation of Hawaii, became president of the republic when the [Provisional Government of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Provisional_Government_of_Hawaii) ended on July 4, 1894.[^cite_note-136][^cite_note-137]

Controversy ensued in the following years as the queen tried to regain her throne. Scholar Lydia Kualapai writes that Liliʻuokalani had "yielded under protest not to the counterfeit Provisional Government of Hawaii but to the superior force of the United States of America" and wrote letters of protest to the president requesting a recognizance of allyship and a reinstatement of her sovereignty against the recent actions of the Provisional Government of Hawaii.[^cite_note-KUALAPAI_2005_32–62-134] Following the January 1893 coup that deposed Liliʻuokalani, many royalists were preparing to overthrow the white-led Republic of Hawaiʻi oligarchy. Hundreds of rifles were covertly shipped to Hawaii and hidden in caves nearby. As armed troops came and went, a Republic of Hawaiʻi patrol discovered the rebel group. On January 6, 1895, gunfire began on both sides and later the rebels were surrounded and captured. Over the next 10 days several skirmishes occurred, until the last armed opposition surrendered or were captured. The Republic of Hawaiʻi took 123 troops into custody as prisoners of war. The mass arrest of nearly 300 more men and women, including Queen Liliʻuokalani, as political prisoners was intended to incapacitate the political resistance against the ruling oligarchy. In March 1895, a military tribunal convicted 170 prisoners of treason and sentenced six troops to be "hung by the neck" until dead, according to historian Ronald Williams Jr. The other prisoners were variously sentenced to from five to thirty-five years' imprisonment at hard labor, while those convicted of lesser charges received sentences from six months' to six years' imprisonment at hard labor.[^cite_note-\:3-138] The queen was sentenced to five years in prison, but spent eight months under house arrest until she was released on parole.[^cite_note-139] The total number of arrests related to the 1895 Kaua Kūloko was 406 people on a summary list of statistics, published by the government of the Republic of Hawaiʻi.[^cite_note-\:3-138]

&#x20;The administration of President [Grover Cleveland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grover_Cleveland) commissioned the [Blount Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blount_Report), which concluded that the removal of Liliʻuokalani had been illegal. Commissioner Blount found the U.S. and its minister guilty on all counts including the overthrow, the landing of the marines, and the recognition of the provisional government.[^cite_note-\:5-130] In a message to Congress, Cleveland wrote:

> And finally, but for the lawless occupation of Honolulu under false pretexts by the United States forces, and but for Minister Stevens' recognition of the provisional government when the United States forces were its sole support and constituted its only military strength, the Queen and her Government would never have yielded to the provisional government, even for a time and for the sole purpose of submitting her case to the enlightened justice of the United States.[^cite_note-\:5-130][^cite_note-\:4-133] By an act of war, committed with the participation of a diplomatic representative of the United States and without authority of Congress, the Government of a feeble but friendly and confiding people has been overthrown. A substantial wrong has thus been done which a due regard for our national character as well as the rights of the injured people requires we should endeavor to repair. The provisional government has not assumed a republican or other constitutional form, but has remained a mere executive council or oligarchy, set up without the assent of the people. It has not sought to find a permanent basis of popular support and has given no evidence of an intention to do so.[^cite_note-\:4-133][^cite_note-\:5-130]

The U.S. government first demanded that Queen Liliʻuokalani be reinstated, but the Provisional Government refused. On December 23, 1893, the response from the Provisional Government of Hawaii, authored by President Sanford B. Dole, was received by Cleveland's representative Minister Albert S. Willis and emphasized that the Provisional Government of Hawaii "unhesitatingly" rejected the demand from the Cleveland Administration.[^cite_note-KUALAPAI_2005_32–62-134]

Congress conducted an independent investigation, and on February 26, 1894, submitted the [Morgan Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Morgan_Report), which found all parties, including Stevens—with the exception of the queen—"not guilty" and not responsible for the coup.[^cite_note-140] Partisans on both sides of the debate questioned the accuracy and impartiality of both the Blount and Morgan reports over the events of 1893.[^cite_note-Adam-127][^cite_note-141][^cite_note-142][^cite_note-HDAICS-143]

In 1993, Congress passed a joint [Apology Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apology_Resolution) regarding the overthrow; it was signed by President [Bill Clinton](3356.md). The resolution apologized and said that the overthrow was illegal in the following phrase: "The Congress—on the occasion of the 100th anniversary of the illegal overthrow of the Kingdom of Hawaiʻi on January 17, 1893, acknowledges the historical significance of this event which resulted in the suppression of the inherent sovereignty of the Native Hawaiian people."[^cite_note-HIgpo-135] The Apology Resolution also "acknowledges that the overthrow of the Kingdom of Hawaiʻi occurred with the active participation of agents and citizens of the United States and further acknowledges that the Native Hawaiian people never directly relinquished to the United States their claims to their inherent sovereignty as a people over their national lands, either through the Kingdom of Hawaiʻi or through a plebiscite or referendum".[^cite_note-HDAICS-143][^cite_note-HIgpo-135]

### Annexation – Territory of Hawaiʻi (1898–1959)

Main articles: [Organic act § List of organic acts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Organic_act#List_of_organic_acts), and [Territory of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Hawaii)



In 1899 Uncle Sam balances his new possessions, which are racistly depicted in the [pickaninny](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pickaninny) stereotype; the figures are [Puerto Rico](23041.md), Hawaii, [Cuba](5042481.md), [Philippines](23440.md) and "Ladrones" (the [Mariana Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mariana_Islands), including [Guam](11974.md)).

After [William McKinley](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_McKinley) won the 1896 U.S. presidential election, advocates pressed to annex the Republic of Hawaiʻi. The previous president, Grover Cleveland, was a friend of Queen Liliʻuokalani. McKinley was open to persuasion by U.S. expansionists and by annexationists from Hawaiʻi. He met with three non-native annexationists: [Lorrin A. Thurston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lorrin_A._Thurston), Francis March Hatch and [William Ansel Kinney](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/William_Ansel_Kinney). After negotiations in June 1897, Secretary of State [John Sherman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Sherman_\(politician\)) agreed to a treaty of annexation with these representatives of the Republic of Hawaiʻi.[^cite_note-144]

The majority of Native Hawaiians vociferously opposed annexation and found a spokesperson in former queen Liliʻuokalani. Opposition crystallized around the [Kūʻē Petition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C5%AB%CA%BB%C4%93_Petitions) drive of 1897, in which 21,269 signatures opposing annexation were procured by members of [Hui Aloha ʻĀina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hui_Aloha_%CA%BB%C4%80ina) (Hawaiian Patriotic League). These petitions were hand-carried to [Washington](108956.md) and delivered to the [United States Senate](24909346.md) by a commission of Native Hawaiian delegates. The petition swayed several key votes and contributed to the Senate's ultimately voting against ratification of the treaty.[^cite_note-145][^cite_note-146]

However, in 1898, the [Newlands Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newlands_Resolution) was introduced in the [US House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_House_of_Representatives). The resolution would legalize the annexation of the Republic of Hawaii to the United States, becoming the [Territory of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Territory_of_Hawaii). The Newlands Resolution was passed by the House on June 15, 1898, by a vote of 209 to 91 and by the Senate on July 6, 1898, by a vote of 42 to 21. It was then signed into law by president McKinley.[^cite_note-147][^cite_note-148][^cite_note-149]

In 1900, Hawaiʻi was granted self-governance and retained [ʻIolani Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iolani_Palace) as the territorial capitol building. Despite several attempts to become a state, Hawaii remained a territory for 60 years. Plantation owners and capitalists, who maintained control through financial institutions such as the [Big Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Five_\(Hawaii\)), found territorial status convenient because they remained able to import cheap, foreign labor. Such immigration and labor practices were prohibited in many states.[^cite_note-150]

The USS Shaw explodes during the Japanese attack on Pearl Harbor.

The Japanese [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor) in 1941 was the primary event which caused the United States to enter [World War II](32927.md).

[Puerto Rican immigration to Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rican_immigration_to_Hawaii) began in 1899, when Puerto Rico's sugar industry was devastated by [a hurricane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1899_San_Ciriaco_hurricane), causing a worldwide shortage of sugar and a huge demand for sugar from Hawaiʻi. Hawaiian [sugarcane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugarcane) [plantation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_plantations_in_Hawaii) owners began to recruit experienced, unemployed laborers in Puerto Rico. Two waves of [Korean immigration to Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_immigration_to_Hawaii) occurred in the 20th century. The first wave arrived between 1903 and 1924; the second wave began in 1965 after President [Lyndon B. Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lyndon_B._Johnson) signed the [Immigration and Nationality Act of 1965](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Immigration_and_Nationality_Act_of_1965), which removed racial and national barriers and resulted in significantly altering the demographic mix in the U.S.[^cite_note-151]

Oʻahu was the target of a surprise [attack on Pearl Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Attack_on_Pearl_Harbor) by the [Empire of Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan) on December 7, 1941. The attack on Pearl Harbor and other military and naval installations, carried out by [aircraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Warplane) and by [midget submarines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midget_submarine), brought the United States into [World War II](32927.md).

### Political changes of 1954 – State of Hawaiʻi (1959–present)

Main articles: [Hawaii Democratic Revolution of 1954](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Democratic_Revolution_of_1954), [Hawaii Admission Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Admission_Act), [Admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Admission_to_the_Union), and [List of U.S. states by date of admission to the Union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_U.S._states_by_date_of_admission_to_the_Union)

See also: [List of Hawaiian sovereignty movement groups § Modern – Sovereignty Organizations (1960s–present)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaiian_sovereignty_movement_groups#Modern_%E2%80%93_Sovereignty_Organizations_\(1960s%E2%80%93present\))

Three young women pack pineapples into cans in 1928.

Prior to the postwar labor movement, Hawaii was governed by plantation owners. Here, three young women pack pineapples into cans in 1928.

In the 1950s, the plantation owners' power was broken by the descendants of immigrant laborers, who were born in Hawaiʻi and were U.S. citizens. They voted against the [Hawaiʻi Republican Party](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Republican_Party), strongly supported by plantation owners. The new majority voted for the [Democratic Party of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democratic_Party_of_Hawai%CA%BBi), which dominated territorial and state politics for more than 40 years. Eager to gain full representation in Congress and the Electoral College, residents actively campaigned for statehood. In Washington, there was talk that Hawaiʻi would be a Republican Party stronghold. As a result, the admission of Hawaii was matched with the admission of Alaska, which was seen as a Democratic Party stronghold. These predictions proved inaccurate; as of 2017, Hawaiʻi almost always votes Democratic, while Alaska typically votes Republican.[^cite_note-152][^cite_note-153][^cite_note-154][^cite_note-155]

During the Cold War, Hawaiʻi became an important site for U.S. [cultural diplomacy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cultural_diplomacy), military training, research, and as a staging ground for the U.S. [war in Vietnam](32611.md).[^cite_note-\:Cheng2-156]<sup>: 105 </sup>

In March 1959, Congress passed the [Hawaiʻi Admissions Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Admission_Act), which U.S. President [Dwight D. Eisenhower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dwight_D._Eisenhower) signed into law.[^cite_note-157] The act excluded [Palmyra Atoll](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palmyra_Atoll) from statehood; it had been part of the Kingdom and Territory of Hawaiʻi. On June 27, 1959, a referendum asked residents of Hawaiʻi to vote on the statehood bill; 94.3% voted in favor of statehood and 5.7% opposed it.[^cite_note-celebrating-50-years-of-statehood-158] The referendum asked voters to choose between accepting the Act and remaining a U.S. territory. The United Nations' [Special Committee on Decolonization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_Committee_on_Decolonization) later removed Hawaiʻi from [its list of non-self-governing territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_list_of_non-self-governing_territories).

After attaining statehood, Hawaiʻi quickly modernized through construction and a rapidly growing tourism economy. Later, state programs promoted Hawaiian culture. The [Hawaiʻi State Constitutional Convention of 1978](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1978_Hawaii_State_Constitutional_Convention) created institutions such as the [Office of Hawaiian Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Hawaiian_Affairs) to promote indigenous language and culture.[^cite_note-159]

## Demographics

### Population

See also: [Hawaii statistical areas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_statistical_areas)

After Europeans and [mainland Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mainland_United_States) first arrived during the [Kingdom of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hawaii) period, the overall population of Hawaii—which until that time composed solely of Indigenous Hawaiians—fell dramatically. Many people of the Indigenous Hawaiian population died to foreign diseases, declining from an estimated 300,000 in the 1770s, to 60,000 in the 1850s, to 24,000 in 1920. Other estimates for the pre-contact population range from 150,000 to 1.5 million.[^cite_note-OHA-21] The population of Hawaii began to finally increase after an influx of primarily Asian settlers that arrived as migrant laborers at the end of the 19th century.[^cite_note-Hawaiian_Encyclopedia-163] In 1923, 42% of the population was of Japanese descent, 9% of Chinese descent, and 16% [Native Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiian) descent.[^cite_note-164]

Hawaii's population has been declining steadily since 2020. The increasing cost of living and needing to import supplies is cited as the top reason for relocating to the [U.S. mainland](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/U.S._mainland), followed by climate change, including more drought, wildfires, as well as rising seas. In 2010, 156,000 residents declared themselves to be solely of Native Hawaiian ancestry, just over half the estimated pre-contact population. An additional 371,000 declared themselves to possess Native Hawaiian ancestry in combination with one or more other races (including other Polynesian groups, but mostly [Asian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asian_people) or [White](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_American)).

In 2018, the [United States Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) estimated the population of Hawaii to be 1,420,491, a decrease of 7,047 from the previous year but an increase of 60,190 (4.42%) since 2010. This includes a natural increase of 48,111 (96,028 births minus 47,917 deaths) and an increase due to net migration of 16,956 people into the state. Immigration from outside the United States resulted in a net increase of 30,068; migration within the country produced a net loss of 13,112 people.[^cite_note-165]

The center of population of Hawaii is located on the island of [Oʻahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O%CA%BBahu). Large numbers of Native Hawaiians have moved to [Las Vegas](47737.md), which has been called the "ninth island" of Hawaii.[^cite_note-166][^cite_note-167]

Hawaii has a *de facto* population of over 1.4 million, due in part to a large number of military personnel and tourist residents. [Oʻahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O%CA%BBahu) is the most populous island, and has the highest population density with a resident population of just under one million in 597 square miles (1,546 km<sup>2</sup>), approximately 1,650 people per square mile.[^cite_note-168][^cite_note-169] Hawaii's 1.4 million residents, spread across 6,000 square miles (15,500 km<sup>2</sup>) of land, result in an average population density of 188.6 persons per square mile.[^cite_note-170] The state has a lower population density than [Ohio](22199.md) and [Illinois](14849.md).[^cite_note-USCB_density-171]

The average projected lifespan of people born in Hawaii in 2000 is 79.8 years; 77.1 years if male, 82.5 if female—longer than the average lifespan of any other U.S. state.[^cite_note-172] As of 2011 the U.S. military reported it had 42,371 personnel on the islands.[^cite_note-Hawaii_mil-173]

According to [HUD](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Department_of_Housing_and_Urban_Development)'s 2022 [Annual Homeless Assessment Report](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Annual_Homeless_Assessment_Report_to_Congress), there were an estimated 5,967 [homeless](19394651.md) people in Hawaii.[^cite_note-174][^cite_note-175]

In 2018, the top countries of origin for immigrants in Hawaii were the [Philippines](23440.md), [China](5405.md), [Japan](15573.md), [Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korea), and the [Marshall Islands](19147.md).[^cite_note-176]

### Ancestry

Further information: [Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiians), [White Americans in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/White_Americans_in_Hawaii), [Filipinos in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipinos_in_Hawaii), and [Japanese in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_in_Hawaii)



[Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_people) immigration to Hawaii was largely fueled by the high demand for plantation labor in Hawaii post-annexation.

Hawaii has the highest percentage of Asian Americans and multiracial Americans and the lowest percentage of White Americans of any state. It is the only state where people who identify as Asian Americans are the largest ethnic group. In 2012, 14.5% of the resident population under age 1 was non-Hispanic white.[^cite_note-185] Hawaii's Asian population consists mainly of 198,000 (14.6%) Filipino Americans, 185,000 (13.6%) Japanese Americans, roughly 55,000 (4.0%) Chinese Americans, and 24,000 (1.8%) [Korean Americans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_American).[^cite_note-US_Census_Bureau-186]

Over 120,000 (8.8%) Hispanic and Latino Americans live in Hawaii. Mexican Americans number over 35,000 (2.6%); Puerto Ricans exceed 44,000 (3.2%). Multiracial Americans constitute almost 25% of Hawaii's population, exceeding 320,000 people. Hawaii is the only state to have a tri-racial group as its largest multiracial group, one that includes white, Asian and Native Hawaiian/Pacific Islander (22% of all multiracial population).[^cite_note-187] The non-Hispanic White population numbers around 310,000—just over 20% of the population. The multi-racial population outnumbers the non-Hispanic white population by about 10,000 people.[^cite_note-US_Census_Bureau-186] In 1970, the Census Bureau reported Hawaii's population was 38.8% white and 57.7% Asian and Pacific Islander.[^cite_note-188]

There are more than 80,000 Indigenous Hawaiians—5.9% of the population.[^cite_note-US_Census_Bureau-186] Including those with partial ancestry, Samoan Americans constitute 2.8% of Hawaii's population, and Tongan Americans constitute 0.6%.[^cite_note-American_FactFinder-189]

The five largest European ancestries in Hawaii are German (7.4%), Irish (5.2%), English (4.6%), Portuguese (4.3%) and Italian (2.7%). About 82.2% of the state's residents were born in the United States. Roughly 75% of foreign-born residents originate from Asia. Hawaii is a [majority-minority state](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majority_minority_in_the_United_States). It was expected to be one of three states that would not have a non-Hispanic white plurality in 2014; the other two are California and [New Mexico](21649.md).[^cite_note-190]
