The third group of foreigners to arrive in Hawaii were from China. Chinese workers on Western trading ships settled in Hawaii starting in 1789. In 1820, the first American missionaries arrived to preach Christianity and teach the Hawaiians Western ways.[^cite_note-Charles_Williams-193] As of 2015, a large proportion of Hawaii's population have Asian ancestry—especially Filipino, Japanese and Chinese. Many are descendants of immigrants brought to work on the sugarcane plantations in the mid-to-late 19th century. The first 153 Japanese immigrants arrived in Hawaii on June 19, 1868. They were not approved by the then-current Japanese government because the contract was between a broker and the [Tokugawa shogunate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tokugawa_shogunate)—by then replaced by the [Meiji Restoration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiji_Restoration). The first Japanese current-government-approved immigrants arrived on February 9, 1885, after Kalākaua's petition to [Emperor Meiji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_Meiji) when Kalākaua visited Japan in 1881.[^cite_note-Goto-194][^cite_note-Wao+Lani-195]

Almost 13,000 Portuguese migrants had arrived by 1899; they also worked on the sugarcane plantations.[^cite_note-Hoffman-196] By 1901, more than 5,000 Puerto Ricans were living in Hawaii.[^cite_note-López-197]

### Languages



Many [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_people) immigrants were [Azorean](3226.md) or [Madeiran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madeira). They brought with them [Catholicism](606848.md) and Portuguese language and cuisine.

English and [Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language) are listed as Hawaii's official languages in the state's 1978 constitution, in Article XV, Section 4.[^cite_note-198] However, the use of Hawaiian is limited because the constitution specifies that "Hawaiian shall be required for public acts and transactions only as provided by law". [Hawaiʻi Creole English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Pidgin), locally referred to as "Pidgin", is the native language of many native residents and is a second language for many others.[^cite_note-199]

The 2000 Census found that 73.4% of Hawaii residents age 5 and older exclusively spoke English at home.[^cite_note-mla-200] According to the 2008 American Community Survey, 74.6% of Hawaii's residents older than 5 spoke only English at home.[^cite_note-factfinder.census.gov-191] In their homes, 21.0% of state residents speak an additional [Asian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Asia), 2.6% speak Spanish, 1.6% speak other [Indo-European languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indo-European_language) and 0.2% speak another language.[^cite_note-factfinder.census.gov-191]

After English, other languages popularly spoken in the state are [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language), [Ilocano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocano_language), and Japanese.[^cite_note-201] 5.4% of residents speak Tagalog, which includes non-native speakers of [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Filipino_language), a Tagalog-based national and co-official language of the Philippines; 5.0% speak Japanese and 4.0% speak Ilocano; 1.2% speak Chinese, 1.7% speak Hawaiian; 1.7% speak Spanish; 1.6% speak [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_language); and 1.0% speak [Samoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoan_language).[^cite_note-mla-200]

#### Hawaiian

Main article: [Hawaiian language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language)

The Hawaiian language has about 2,000 native speakers, about 0.15% of the total population.[^cite_note-Lyovin-202] According to the [United States Census](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census), there were more than 24,000 total speakers of the language in Hawaii in 2006–2008.[^cite_note-Census_Bureau_Hawaiian_Speaker_estimate-203] Hawaiian is a Polynesian member of the [Austronesian language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_languages).[^cite_note-Lyovin-202] It is closely related to other [Polynesian languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_languages), such as [Marquesan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquesan_language), [Tahitian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahitian_language), [Māori](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81ori_language), [Rapa Nui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapa_Nui_language) (the language of [Easter Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Easter_Island)), and less closely to [Samoan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Samoan_language) and [Tongan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tongan_language).[^cite_note-204]

According to Schütz, the Marquesans colonized the archipelago in roughly 300 CE[^cite_note-205] and were later followed by waves of seafarers from the [Society Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Society_Islands), [Samoa](27238.md) and [Tonga](30158.md).[^cite_note-206] These Polynesians remained in the islands; they eventually became the Hawaiian people and [their languages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language#Family_and_origin) evolved into the Hawaiian language.[^cite_note-207] Kimura and Wilson say: "\[l]inguists agree that Hawaiian is closely related to Eastern Polynesian, with a particularly strong link in the Southern Marquesas, and a secondary link in Tahiti, which may be explained by voyaging between the Hawaiian and Society Islands".[^cite_note-208]

Before the arrival of Captain James Cook, the Hawaiian language had no written form. That form was developed mainly by American Protestant missionaries between 1820 and 1826 who assigned to the Hawaiian phonemes letters from the Latin alphabet. Interest in Hawaiian increased significantly in the late 20th century. With the help of the Office of Hawaiian Affairs, specially designated immersion schools in which all subjects would be taught in Hawaiian were established. The [University of Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi) developed a Hawaiian-language graduate studies program. Municipal codes were altered to favor Hawaiian place and street names for new civic developments.[^cite_note-209]

Hawaiian distinguishes between [long and short vowel sounds](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vowel_length). In modern practice, vowel length is indicated with a [macron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macron_\(diacritic\)) (*[kahakō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language#Orthography)*). Hawaiian-language newspapers (*nūpepa*) published from 1834 to 1948 and traditional native speakers of Hawaiian generally omit the marks in their own writing. The ʻokina and kahakō are intended to capture the proper pronunciation of Hawaiian words.[^cite_note-210] The Hawaiian language uses the [glottal stop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Glottal_stop) (*[ʻOkina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%CA%BBOkina)*) as a consonant. It is written as a symbol similar to the apostrophe or left-hanging (opening) single quotation mark.[^cite_note-211]

The keyboard layout used for Hawaiian is [QWERTY](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/QWERTY).[^cite_note-212]

#### Hawaiian Pidgin

Main article: [Hawaiian Pidgin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Pidgin)



Mixed Hawaiian/European-American family in Honolulu, 1850s

**Hawaiian Pidgin**, officially known as [Hawaiʻi Creole English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Pidgin) (HCE), is a creole language that emerged in [Hawaiʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_\(island\)) during the 19th century as a means of communication among diverse groups working on sugarcane plantations.[^cite_note-\:7-213] Its lexicon is primarily derived from [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language), with significant contributions from [Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_language), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_language), [Japanese](15606.md), [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language), [Ilocano](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ilocano_language), and [Tagalog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tagalog_language).

The development of Hawaiian Pidgin began with [Pidgin Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pidgin_Hawaiian), an earlier pidgin that formed in the 1790s during initial contact between [Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiians) and foreigners. As plantation laborers from various countries arrived, a new pidgin based on English evolved to facilitate communication among workers and supervisors.[^cite_note-214]

By the early 20th century, children of these plantation workers began acquiring **Hawaiian Pidgin** as their first language, leading to its creolization. This transition marked the emergence of HCE as a fully developed creole language.[^cite_note-215][^cite_note-\:7-213]

HCE incorporates Hawaiian words, especially in place names and terms for local flora and fauna. For instance, the Hawaiian term for tuna, "ahi," is commonly used in HCE. Additionally, certain English words have adapted meanings; "aunty" and "uncle" are used to address any respected elder, regardless of familial relation.[^cite_note-\:8-216][^cite_note-217][^cite_note-218]

Some expressions from HCE have permeated other communities, particularly through surfing culture. Terms like "brah" (brother) and "da kine" (a versatile placeholder term) have gained recognition beyond Hawaiʻi.[^cite_note-\:8-216]

In 2015, the [U.S. Census Bureau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Census_Bureau) recognized Hawaiian Pidgin as an official language in Hawaiʻi, reflecting its widespread use among residents. Despite this recognition, debates continue about its role in education and its impact on learning [Standard English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Standard_English).[^cite_note-219]

#### Hawaiʻi Sign Language

[Hawaiʻi Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawai%CA%BBi_Sign_Language), a [sign language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sign_language) for the Deaf based on the Hawaiian language, has been in use in the islands since the early 1800s. It is dwindling in numbers due to [American Sign Language](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Sign_Language) supplanting HSL through schooling and various other domains.[^cite_note-220]

Hawaii is among the most religiously diverse states in the U.S., with one in ten residents practicing a non-Christian faith.[^cite_note-223] Roughly one-quarter to half the population identify as unaffiliated and nonreligious, making Hawaii one of the most secular states as well.

Christianity remains the majority religion, represented mainly by various [Protestant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants) groups and [Catholicism](606848.md). The second-largest religion is [Buddhism](3267529.md), which comprises a larger proportion of the population than in any other state; it is concentrated in the Japanese community. Native Hawaiians continue to engage in traditional religious and spiritual practices today, often adhering to Christian and traditional beliefs at the same time.[^cite_note-LaDuke_1999-71][^cite_note-FLEXNER_2016_307–331-112][^cite_note-People_of_the_seventh_fire-100][^cite_note-nps.gov-113]

The [Cathedral Church of Saint Andrew](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_Church_of_Saint_Andrew_\(Honolulu\)) in Honolulu was formally the seat of the [Hawaiian Reformed Catholic Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Hawaii), a province of the [Anglican Communion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglican_Communion) that had been the state church of the Kingdom of Hawaii; it subsequently merged into the [Episcopal Church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Church_\(USA\)) in the 1890s following the overthrow of the Kingdom of Hawaii, becoming the seat of the [Episcopal Diocese of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Episcopal_Diocese_of_Hawaii). The [Cathedral Basilica of Our Lady of Peace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_Basilica_of_Our_Lady_of_Peace) and the [Co-Cathedral of Saint Theresa of the Child Jesus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-Cathedral_of_Saint_Theresa_of_the_Child_Jesus_\(Honolulu,_Hawaii\)) serve as seats of the [Diocese of Honolulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Honolulu). The Eastern Orthodox community is centered around the [Saints Constantine and Helen Greek Orthodox Cathedral of the Pacific](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saints_Constantine_and_Helen_Greek_Orthodox_Cathedral_of_the_Pacific_\(Honolulu\)).

The largest religious denominations by membership were the Catholic Church with 249,619 adherents in 2010;\[^cite_note-[www.thearda.com-224](http://www.thearda.com-224/)] the [Church of Jesus Christ of Latter-day Saints](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Church_of_Jesus_Christ_of_Latter-day_Saints_in_Hawaii) with 68,128 adherents in 2009;[^cite_note-225] the [United Church of Christ](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Church_of_Christ) with 115 congregations and 20,000 members; and the [Southern Baptist Convention](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southern_Baptist_Convention) with 108 congregations and 18,000 members.[^cite_note-226] [Nondenominational churches](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nondenominational_church) collectively have 128 congregations and 32,000 members.

According to data provided by religious establishments, religion in Hawaii in 2000 was distributed as follows:[^cite_note-227][^cite_note-228]

- Christianity: 351,000 (29%)

- Buddhism: 110,000 (9%)

- Judaism: 10,000 (1%)

  [^cite_note-229]

- Other: 100,000 (10%)

- Unaffiliated: 650,000 (51%)

### LGBTQ people

See also: [LGBTQ rights in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQ_rights_in_Hawaii)

Hawaii has had a long history of [LGBTQIA+](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBTQIA+) identities. *[Māhū](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C4%81h%C5%AB)* ("in the middle") were a precolonial [third gender](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_gender) with traditional spiritual and social roles, widely respected as healers. Homosexual relationships known as *[aikāne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aik%C4%81ne)* were widespread and normal in ancient Hawaiian society.[^cite_note-Murray2002-243][^cite_note-Kornblum2011-244][^cite_note-Klarman2012-245] Among men, *aikāne* relationships often began as teens and continued throughout their adult lives, even if they also maintained heterosexual partners.[^cite_note-EmberEmber2003-246] While *aikāne* usually refers to male homosexuality, some stories also refer to women, implying that women may have been involved in *aikāne* relationships as well.[^cite_note-Zimmerman2000-247] Journals written by [Captain Cook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_Cook)'s crew record that many *[aliʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ali%CA%BBi)* (hereditary nobles) also engaged in *aikāne* relationships, and [Kamehameha the Great](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamehameha_I), the founder and first ruler of the [Kingdom of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hawaii), was also known to participate. Cook's second lieutenant and co-astronomer [James King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/James_King_\(Royal_Navy_officer\)) observed that "all the chiefs had them", and recounts that Cook was actually asked by one chief to leave King behind, considering the role a great honor.

Hawaiian scholar [Lilikalā Kameʻeleihiwa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lilikal%C4%81_Kame%CA%BBeleihiwa) notes that *aikāne* served a practical purpose of building mutual trust and cohesion; "If you didn't sleep with a man, how could you trust him when you went into battle? How would you know if he was going to be the warrior that would protect you at all costs, if he wasn't your lover?"[^cite_note-mahu-248]

As Western colonial influences intensified in the late 19th and early 20th century, the word *aikāne* was [expurgated](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expurgation) of its original sexual meaning, and in print simply meant "friend". Nonetheless, in Hawaiian language publications its metaphorical meaning can still mean either "friend" or "lover" without stigmatization.[^cite_note-249]

A 2023 [Williams Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Williams_Institute) analysis using 2020–2021 CDC data estimated that 5.1% of Hawaiʻi adults—approximately 56,900 people—identify as LGBT. Same-sex couple households have increased substantially since 2010: the 2023 [American Community Survey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/American_Community_Survey) estimated 5,918 same-sex couple households in Hawaiʻi, compared with 3,239 in 2010.[^cite_note-250] Of the 2023 households, 3,663 (61.9%) were married same-sex couples, reflecting the nationwide expansion of marriage equality following the 2015 *[Obergefell v. Hodges](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Obergefell_v._Hodges)* decision.[^cite_note-251] In 2013, Hawaii became the fifteenth U.S. state to legalize same-sex marriage; this reportedly boosted tourism by \$217 million.[^cite_note-252]

## Economy

See also: [Agriculture in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Hawaii), [Sugar plantations in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugar_plantations_in_Hawaii), [Big Five (Hawaii)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Five_\(Hawaii\)), and [Plantation economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plantation_economy)

In a pineapple field, a laborer stands with his hat in hand.

Post-annexation, Hawaii's economy and demographic changes were shaped mostly by growth in the agricultural sector.

A painting of two white women surfing, circa 1935.

From the end of World War II onwards, depictions and photographs, such as this one of Hawaii as a tropical, leisure paradise, encouraged the growth of tourism in Hawaii, which eventually became the largest industry of the islands.

An American soldier at Schofield Barracks.

The U.S. federal government's spending on Hawaii-stationed personnel, installations, and materiel (either directly or through military personnel spending) amounts to Hawaii's second largest source of income after tourism.

The history of Hawaii's economy can be traced through a succession of dominant industries: [sandalwood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandalwood),[^cite_note-253] [whaling](33557.md),[^cite_note-254] sugarcane, [pineapple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pineapple), the military, tourism and education. By the 1840s, sugar plantations had gained a strong foothold in the Hawaiian economy, due to a high demand of sugar in the United States and rapid transport via steamships.[^cite_note-MacLennan_2004_37–62-87] Sugarcane plantations were tightly controlled by American missionary families and businessmen known as "[the Big Five](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_Five_\(Hawaii\))", who monopolized control of the sugar industry's profits.[^cite_note-MacLennan_2004_37–62-87][^cite_note-Huard-88] By the time Hawaiian annexation was being considered in 1898, sugarcane producers turned to cultivating tropical fruits like pineapple, which became the principal export for Hawaii's plantation economy.[^cite_note-Huard-88][^cite_note-MacLennan_2004_37–62-87]

Since statehood in 1959, tourism has been the largest industry, contributing 24.3% of the gross state product (GSP) in 1997, despite efforts to diversify. In 2025, Hawaii's gross state product was \$124.6 billion and the per capita personal income for Hawaii residents was \$76,592.[^cite_note-255] Hawaiian exports include food and clothing. These industries play a small role in the Hawaiian economy, due to the shipping distance to viable markets, such as the [West Coast of the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Coast_of_the_United_States). The state's food exports include coffee, macadamia nuts, pineapple, livestock, sugarcane, and honey.[^cite_note-256]

By weight, honey bees may be the state's most valuable export in 2012.[^cite_note-257] According to the Hawaii Agricultural Statistics Service, in 2002 agricultural sales were US\$370.9 million from diversified agriculture, US\$100.6 million from pineapple,[^cite_note-258] and US\$64.3 million from sugarcane.[^cite_note-259] Hawaii's relatively consistent climate has attracted the seed industry, which is able to test three generations of crops per year on the islands, compared with one or two on the mainland.[^cite_note-260] Seeds yielded US\$264 million in 2012, supporting 1,400 workers.[^cite_note-ny1310-261]

As of August 2025, the state's unemployment rate was 2.7%.[^cite_note-\:9-262] The historically lowest unemployment rate of 2.0% was recorded in December 2017, and its highest, 22.0%, in April 2020.[^cite_note-\:9-262] In 2025, 99.3% of Hawaii's businesses were [small businesses](276495.md), employing 49.6% of the state's workforce.[^cite_note-263]

In 2023, approximately \$10.2 billion in defense spending flowed into Hawaiʻi, equivalent to about 9.2% of the state's GDP. The military presence included approximately 73,000 personnel, of whom 43,118 were active-duty service members.[^cite_note-264] According to a 2013 study by Phoenix Marketing International, Hawaii at that time had the fourth-largest number of millionaires per capita in the United States, with a ratio of 7.2%.[^cite_note-265]

### Taxation

Tax is collected by the Hawaii Department of Taxation.[^cite_note-266] Most government revenue comes from [personal income taxes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Income_tax) and a [general excise tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gross_receipts_tax) (GET) levied primarily on businesses; there is no statewide tax on sales,[^cite_note-267] personal property, or stock transfers,[^cite_note-268] while the effective property tax rate is among the lowest in the country.[^cite_note-269] The high rate of tourism means that millions of visitors generate public revenue through GET and the hotel room tax.[^cite_note-taxes-270] However, Hawaii residents generally pay among the most state taxes per person in the U.S.[^cite_note-taxes-270]

The Tax Foundation of Hawaii considers the state's tax burden too high, claiming that it contributes to higher prices and the perception of an unfriendly business climate.[^cite_note-taxes-270] The nonprofit Tax Foundation ranks Hawaii third in income tax burden and second in its overall tax burden, though notes that a significant portion of taxes are borne by tourists.[^cite_note-271] Former [State Senator](798044.md) [Sam Slom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sam_Slom) attributed Hawaii's comparatively high tax rate to the fact that the state government is responsible for education, health care, and social services that are usually handled at a county or municipal level in most other states.[^cite_note-taxes-270]

### Cost of living

The cost of living in Hawaii, specifically Honolulu, is high compared to that of most major U.S. cities, although it is 6.7% lower than in New York City and 3.6% lower than in San Francisco.[^cite_note-272] These numbers may not take into account some costs, such as increased travel costs for flights, additional shipping fees, and the loss of promotional participation opportunities for customers outside the contiguous U.S. While some online stores offer free shipping on orders to Hawaii, many merchants exclude Hawaii, Alaska, Puerto Rico and certain other U.S. territories.[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274]



Hawaii electricity production by type



Electricity prices by metro area

[Hawaiian Electric Industries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Electric_Industries), a privately owned company, provides 95% of the state's population with electricity. Although the state's electricity system remains heavily dependent on imported petroleum, renewable sources accounted for 36.8% of Hawaiian Electric's generation in 2025, up from 36% in 2024.[^cite_note-275] Hawaii also continues to have the highest electricity prices in the United States: in 2024, the average retail price across all sectors was 38.00 cents per kilowatt-hour, compared with 12.94 cents nationally.[^cite_note-276] Preliminary data for the first quarter of 2026 put Hawaii's average at 37.15 cents per kilowatt-hour, compared with 14.23 cents nationally.[^cite_note-277]



Median housing prices by State

The median home value in Hawaii in the 2000 U.S. Census was US\$272,700, while the national median home value was US\$119,600. Hawaii home values were the highest of all states, including California with a median home value of US\$211,500.[^cite_note-278] Research from the [National Association of Realtors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Association_of_Realtors) places the 2010 median sale price of a single family home in Honolulu, Hawaii, at US\$607,600 and the U.S. median sales price at US\$173,200. The sale price of single family homes in Hawaii was the highest of any U.S. city in 2010, just above that of the Silicon Valley area of California (US\$602,000).[^cite_note-279]

Hawaii's very high [cost of living](522934.md) is the result of several interwoven factors of the global economy in addition to domestic U.S. government trade policy. Like other regions with desirable weather year-round, such as California, [Arizona](21883824.md) and Florida, Hawaii's residents can be considered to be subject to a "[sunshine tax](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunshine_tax)". This situation is further exacerbated by the natural factors of geography and world distribution that lead to higher prices for goods due to increased shipping costs, a problem which many [island states and territories](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Island_country) suffer from as well.

The higher costs to ship goods across an ocean may be further increased by the requirements of the [Jones Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merchant_Marine_Act_of_1920), which generally requires that goods be transported between places within the U.S., including between the mainland U.S. west coast and Hawaii, using only U.S.-owned, built, and crewed ships. Jones Act-compliant vessels are often more expensive to build and operate than foreign equivalents, which can drive up shipping costs. While the Jones Act does not affect transportation of goods to Hawaii directly from Asia, this type of trade is nonetheless not common; this is a result of other primarily economic reasons including additional costs associated with stopping over in Hawaii (e.g. pilot and port fees), the market size of Hawaii, and the economics of using ever-larger ships that cannot be handled in Hawaii for transoceanic voyages. Therefore, Hawaii relies on receiving most inbound goods on Jones Act-qualified vessels originating from the U.S. west coast, which may contribute to the increased cost of some consumer goods and therefore the overall cost of living.[^cite_note-Keeping_up_with_the_Jones_Act-280][^cite_note-U.S._Law_Restricting_Foreign_Ships_Leads_to_Higher_Gas_Prices-281] Critics of the Jones Act contend that Hawaii consumers ultimately bear the expense of transporting goods imposed by the Jones Act.[^cite_note-Star-Bulletin_on_Jones_Act-282]

## Culture

Main article: [Culture of the Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_the_Native_Hawaiians)

The aboriginal culture of Hawaii is Polynesian. Hawaii represents the northernmost extension of the vast [Polynesian Triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_Triangle) of the south and central Pacific Ocean. While traditional Hawaiian culture remains as vestiges in modern Hawaiian society, there are re-enactments of the ceremonies and traditions throughout the islands. Some of these cultural influences, including the popularity (in greatly modified form) of *[lūʻau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luau)* and *[hula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hula)*, and gestures such as the [Shaka sign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaka_sign), are strong enough to affect the wider United States.

### Cuisine

Main article: [Cuisine of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_Hawaii)

A painting of a man carrying taro by a yoke.

[Taro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taro), or in Hawaiian *kalo*, was one of the primary staples in Ancient Hawaii and remains a central ingredient in Hawaiian gastronomy today.

The [cuisine of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_Hawaii) is a fusion of many foods brought by immigrants to the Hawaiian Islands, including the earliest Polynesians and [Native Hawaiian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiian_cuisine), and [American](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_the_United_States), [Chinese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chinese_cuisine), [Filipino](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philippine_cuisine), [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_cuisine), [Korean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Korean_cuisine), [Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_cuisine), [Puerto Rican](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Puerto_Rican_cuisine), and [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_cuisine) origins. Plant and animal food sources are imported from around the world for agricultural use in Hawaii. *[Poi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Poi_\(Hawaiian_food\))*, a starch made by pounding [taro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Taro), is one of the traditional foods of the islands. Many local restaurants serve the ubiquitous [plate lunch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Plate_lunch), which features two scoops of rice, a simplified version of American [macaroni salad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Macaroni_salad) and a variety of toppings including [hamburger](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hamburger) patties, a fried egg, and [gravy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gravy) of a *[loco moco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Loco_moco)*, Japanese style *[tonkatsu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tonkatsu)* or the traditional lūʻau favorites, including *[kālua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalua)* pork and *[laulau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laulau)*. *[Spam musubi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spam_musubi)* is an example of the fusion of ethnic cuisine that developed on the islands among the mix of immigrant groups and military personnel. In the 1990s, a group of chefs developed [Hawaii regional cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_regional_cuisine) as a contemporary fusion cuisine.

### Customs and etiquette

Main article: [Customs and etiquette in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Customs_and_etiquette_in_Hawaii)

Some key customs and etiquette in Hawaii are as follows: when visiting a home, it is considered good manners to bring a small gift for one's host (for example, a dessert). Thus, parties are usually in the form of [potlucks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Potluck). Most locals take their shoes off before entering a home. It is customary for Hawaiian families, regardless of ethnicity, to hold a luau to celebrate a child's first birthday. It is also customary at Hawaiian weddings, especially at Filipino weddings, for the bride and groom to do a money dance (also called the [pandanggo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pandanggo)). Print media and local residents recommend that one refer to residents of Hawaii who are not ethnically Hawaiian as "locals of Hawaii" or "people of Hawaii".

### Hawaiian mythology

Main article: [Hawaiian mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_mythology)



A stone carving of a Hawaiian deity, housed at a [German](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Germany) museum

Hawaiian mythology includes the legends, historical tales, and sayings of the ancient Hawaiian people. It is considered a variant of a more general [Polynesian mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_mythology) that developed a unique character for several centuries before c. 1800. It is associated with the [Hawaiian religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_religion), which was officially suppressed in the 19th century but was kept alive by some practitioners to the modern day.[^cite_note-283] Prominent figures and terms include [Aumakua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aumakua), the spirit of an ancestor or family god and [Kāne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K%C4%81ne), the highest of the four major Hawaiian deities.

### Polynesian mythology

Main article: [Polynesian mythology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_mythology)



A sacred god figure wrapping for the war god [ʻOro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/'Oro), made of woven dried coconut fibre ([sennit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sennit)), made to protect a Polynesian god effigy (*toʻo*), carved from wood

Polynesian mythology is the [oral traditions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oral_tradition) of the people of Polynesia, a grouping of Central and South Pacific Ocean island [archipelagos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archipelago) in the [Polynesian triangle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_triangle) together with the scattered cultures known as the [Polynesian outliers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_outliers). Polynesians speak languages that descend from a language reconstructed as [Proto-Polynesian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proto-Polynesian_language) that was probably spoken in the area around [Tonga](30158.md) and [Samoa](27238.md) in around 1000 BC.[^cite_note-284]

Prior to the 15th century, [Polynesian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_culture) migrated east to the [Cook Islands](7067.md), and from there to other island groups such as Tahiti and the [Marquesas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marquesas). Their descendants later discovered the islands [Tahiti](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tahiti), [Rapa Nui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapa_Nui), and later the Hawaiian Islands and [New Zealand](4913064.md).[^cite_note-285]

The Polynesian languages are part of the [Austronesian language family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Austronesian_language_family). Many are close enough in terms of vocabulary and grammar to be [mutually intelligible](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mutual_intelligibility). There are also substantial cultural similarities between the various groups, especially in terms of social organization, childrearing, horticulture, building and textile technologies. Their mythologies in particular demonstrate local reworkings of commonly shared tales. The Polynesian cultures each have distinct but related oral traditions; legends or myths are traditionally considered to recount ancient history (the time of "pō") and the adventures of gods ("[atua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atua)") and deified ancestors.

### List of state parks

Main article: [List of Hawaiian state parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaiian_state_parks)

There are [many Hawaiian state parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaiian_state_parks).

- The [Island of Hawaiʻi](70962.md) has state parks, recreation areas, and historical parks.

- [Kauaʻi](70961.md) has the Ahukini State Recreation Pier, six state parks, and the [Russian Fort Elizabeth State Historical Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russian_Fort_Elizabeth_State_Historical_Park).

- [Maui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maui) has two state monuments, several state parks, and the [Polipoli Spring State Recreation Area](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polipoli_Spring_State_Recreation_Area). [Molokaʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moloka'i) has the [Palaʻau State Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pala'au_State_Park).

- [Oʻahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O%CA%BBahu) has several state parks, a number of state recreation areas, and a number of monuments, including the [Ulu Pō Heiau State Monument](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulu_P%C5%8D_Heiau_State_Monument).

### Literature

Main article: [Literature in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Literature_in_Hawaii)

The literature of Hawaii is diverse and includes authors [Kiana Davenport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kiana_Davenport), [Lois-Ann Yamanaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lois-Ann_Yamanaka), and [Kaui Hart Hemmings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaui_Hart_Hemmings). Hawaiian magazines include *[Hana Hou!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hana_Hou!)*, *[Hawaii Business](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Business)* and *[Honolulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_\(magazine\))*, among others.

### Music

Main article: [Music of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Music_of_Hawaii)



Different types of [ukuleles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ukulele), widely used in Hawaiian music



[Jack Johnson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jack_Johnson_\(musician\)), a folk rock musician, was born and raised on [Oʻahu's North Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Shore_\(O%CA%BBahu\)).

The music of Hawaii includes traditional and popular styles, ranging from native Hawaiian folk music to modern rock and [hip-hop](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hip-hop).

Styles such as [slack-key guitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slack-key_guitar) are well known worldwide, while Hawaiian-tinged music is a frequent part of [Hollywood](418334.md) soundtracks. Hawaii also made a major contribution to [country music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Country_music) with the introduction of the [steel guitar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steel_guitar).[^cite_note-Unterberger-286]

Traditional Hawaiian folk music is a major part of the state's musical heritage. The Hawaiian people have inhabited the islands for centuries and have retained much of their traditional musical knowledge. Their music is largely religious in nature, and includes chanting and dance music.

Hawaiian music has had an enormous impact on the [music of other Polynesian islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_music); according to Peter Manuel, the influence of Hawaiian music is a "unifying factor in the development of modern Pacific musics".[^cite_note-Manuel-287] Native Hawaiian musician and Hawaiian sovereignty activist [Israel Kamakawiwoʻole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Israel_Kamakawiwo%CA%BBole), famous for his medley of "[Somewhere Over the Rainbow/What a Wonderful World](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Somewhere_Over_the_Rainbow/What_a_Wonderful_World)", was named "The Voice of Hawaii" by [NPR](29697232.md) in 2010 in its 50 great voices series.[^cite_note-288]

### Sports

Due to its distance from the continental United States, team sports in Hawaii are characterised by youth, collegial and amateur teams over professional teams, although some professional teams sports teams have at one time played in the state. Notable professional teams include [The Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hawaiians_\(WFL\)), which played at the [World Football League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Football_League) in 1974 and 1975; the [Hawaii Islanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Islanders), a Triple-A minor league baseball team that played at the [Pacific Coast League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Coast_League) from 1961 to 1987; and [Team Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Team_Hawaii), a [North American Soccer League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_American_Soccer_League_\(1968%E2%80%9384\)) team that played in 1977.

Notable college sports events in Hawaii include the [Maui Invitational Tournament](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maui_Invitational_Tournament), [Diamond Head Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diamond_Head_Classic) (basketball) and [Hawaii Bowl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Bowl) (football). The only [NCAA Division I](669931.md) team in Hawaii is the [Hawaii Rainbow Warriors and Rainbow Wahine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Rainbow_Warriors_and_Rainbow_Wahine), which competes at the [Big West Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Big_West_Conference) (major sports), [Mountain West Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_West_Conference) (football) and [Mountain Pacific Sports Federation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mountain_Pacific_Sports_Federation) (minor sports). There are three teams in NCAA Division II: [Chaminade Silverswords](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaminade_Silverswords), [Hawaii Pacific Sharks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Pacific_Sharks) and Hawaii-Hilo Vulcans, all of which compete at the [Pacific West Conference](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_West_Conference).

Surfing at [North Shore](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North_Shore_\(O%CA%BBahu\)) of [Oʻahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/O%CA%BBahu)

[Surfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surfing) has been a central part of [Polynesian culture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polynesian_culture) for centuries. Since the late 19th century, Hawaii has become a major site for surfists from around the world. Notable competitions include the [Triple Crown of Surfing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Triple_Crown_of_Surfing) and [The Eddie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Eddie). Likewise, Hawaii has produced elite-level swimmers, including five-time Olympic medalist [Duke Kahanamoku](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duke_Kahanamoku) and [Buster Crabbe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Buster_Crabbe), who set 16 swimming world records.

Hawaii has hosted the [Sony Championship Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sony_Championship_Hawaii) golf tournament since 1965, the [Lotte Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lotte_Championship) golf tournament since 2012, the [Tournament of Champions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tournament_of_Champions_\(golf\)) golf tournament from 1999 to 2025, the [Honolulu Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_Marathon) since 1973, the [Ironman World Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ironman_World_Championship) triathlon race since 1978, the [Ultraman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ultraman_\(endurance_challenge\)) triathlon since 1983, the [National Football League](21211.md)'s [Pro Bowl](274117.md) from 1980 to 2016, the [2000 FINA World Open Water Swimming Championships](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2000_FINA_World_Open_Water_Swimming_Championships), and the [2008 Pan-Pacific Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_Pan-Pacific_Championship) and [2012 Hawaiian Islands Invitational](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_Hawaiian_Islands_Invitational) soccer tournaments.

Hawaii has produced a number of notable [Mixed Martial Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mixed_Martial_Arts) fighters, such as former [UFC Lightweight Champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UFC_Lightweight_Champion) and [UFC Welterweight Champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UFC_Welterweight_Champion) [B.J. Penn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/B.J._Penn), and former [UFC Featherweight Champion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UFC_Featherweight_Champion) [Max Holloway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Max_Holloway). Other notable Hawaiian Martial Artists include [Travis Browne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Travis_Browne), [K. J. Noons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/K._J._Noons), [Brad Tavares](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brad_Tavares) and [Wesley Correira](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wesley_Correira).

Hawaiians have found success in the world of [sumo wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sumo_wrestling). [Takamiyama Daigorō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Takamiyama_Daigor%C5%8D) was the first foreigner to ever win a sumo title in Japan, while his protege [Akebono Tarō](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akebono_Tar%C5%8D) became a top-level sumo wrestler in Japan during the 1990s before transitioning into a successful [professional wrestling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional_wrestling) career in the 2000s. Akebono was the first foreign-born Sumo to reach [Yokozuna](985879.md) in history and helped fuel a boom in interest in Sumo during his career.

## Tourism

|  | This section **may be [unbalanced](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia\:Neutral_point_of_view#Undue_weight) toward certain viewpoints**. Please help [improve it](https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Hawaii\&action=edit) by adding information on neglected viewpoints. Relevant discussion may be found on the [talk page](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Talk\:Hawaii). *(April 2025)* |
| --- | --- |

Main article: [Tourism in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Hawaii)



Tourism is Hawaii's leading employment sector, providing numerous jobs in places such as [Waikīkī](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waik%C4%ABk%C4%AB) in [Honolulu](13887.md) (above).



[Punaluʻu Beach](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punalu%CA%BBu_Beach), on the Big Island features black volcanic sand.

Tourism is an important part of the Hawaiian economy as it represents a quarter of the economy. According to the Hawaii Department of Business, Economic Development & Tourism (DBEDT) and the Hawaii Tourism Authority, a total of 9,642,991 visitors arrived in 2025 which decreased 0.6% from the previous year. However, visitor spending reached a record approximately \$21.75 billion in 2025, an increase of 5.7% from 2024.[^cite_note-\:10-289] Due to mild year-round weather, tourist travel is popular throughout the year. Tourism supported approximately 222,000 jobs statewide and generated about \$3.04 billion in state tax revenue.[^cite_note-290] The [U.S. West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Western_United_States) was Hawaii's largest visitor market, accounting for 5.01 million arrivals, followed by the [U.S. East](509486.md) with 2.41 million. Japan accounted for approximately 732,000 visitors, while Canada accounted for approximately 394,000.[^cite_note-\:10-289]

It was with statehood in 1959 that the Hawaii tourism industry began to grow.[^cite_note-\:6-291]

According to Hawaiian scholar [Haunani-Kay Trask](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haunani-Kay_Trask), tourism in Hawaii has led to the commodification and exploitation of Hawaiian culture resulting in insidious forms of "cultural prostitution". Hawaii has been used to fuel ideas of escapism yet tourism in Hawaii ignores the harm Kanaka and locals experience.[^cite_note-TraskBL-292] Cultural traditions such as the hula have been made "ornamental ... a form of exotica" for tourists as a way for large corporations and land owners to gain profit over the exploitation of Hawaiian people and culture.[^cite_note-TraskBL-292]

Tourism in Hawaii has been considered as an escape from reality resulting in the dismissal of violence faced by Native Hawaiians and locals living on the land. According to scholar [Winona LaDuke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winona_LaDuke), native Hawaiians have been forced to gather "shrimp and fish from ponds sitting on resort property".[^cite_note-293] Tourism has also had damaging effects on the environment such as water shortages, overcrowding, sea level rising, elevated sea surface temperatures and micro plastics on beaches.[^cite_note-294][^cite_note-295][^cite_note-296]

Due to the [COVID-19 pandemic](62750956.md), tourism in Hawaii came to a halt, which allowed the land, water, and animals to began to heal. Fish such as the baby akule and big ulua returned after years of not being around the bay. The coral reefs, fish, water growth, and [limu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Limu_\(algae\)) (algae) growth was able to flourish without the impact of tourism.[^cite_note-297]

There has been pushback against tourism by Native Hawaiians, urging people not to visit the islands. A survey by the Hawaii Tourism Authority indicated over two-thirds of Hawaiians did not want tourists to return to Hawaii. Tourism had "become extractive and hurtful, with tourists coming here and taking, taking, taking, taking, without any reciprocation with locals".[^cite_note-298]

Hawaii hosts numerous cultural events. The annual [Merrie Monarch Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merrie_Monarch_Festival) is an international Hula competition.[^cite_note-299] The [Hawaii International Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_International_Film_Festival) is the premier film festival for Pacific rim cinema.[^cite_note-Shane_Nelson-300] Honolulu hosts the state's long-running LGBT film festival, the [Honolulu Rainbow Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_Rainbow_Film_Festival).[^cite_note-301][^cite_note-302]

## Health

Main article: [Hawaii Prepaid Health Care Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Prepaid_Health_Care_Act)

As of 2009, Hawaii's health care system insures 92% of residents. Under the state's plan, businesses are required to provide insurance to employees who work more than twenty hours per week. Heavy regulation of insurance companies helps reduce the cost to employers. Due in part to heavy emphasis on preventive care, Hawaiians' total health care expenses measured as a percentage of state GDP are substantially lower.[^cite_note-303] Proponents of [universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_health_care) elsewhere in the U.S. sometimes use Hawaii as a model for proposed federal and state health care plans.[^cite_note-304]

## Education

### Public schools

Main article: [Hawaii Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Department_of_Education)

See also: [List of elementary schools in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_elementary_schools_in_Hawaii), [List of middle schools in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_middle_schools_in_Hawaii), and [List of high schools in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_high_schools_in_Hawaii)

Façade of a public high school.

[Waianae High School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waianae_High_School), located in [Waiʻanae](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waianae,_Hawaii), houses an educational community media center

Hawaii has the only school system within the U.S. that is unified statewide. Policy decisions are made by the fourteen-member state [Board of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Board_of_Education), which sets policy and hires the superintendent of schools, who oversees the [Hawaii Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Department_of_Education). The Department of Education is divided into seven districts; four on Oʻahu and one for each of the other three counties.

Public elementary, middle and high school test scores in Hawaii are below national averages on tests mandated under the [No Child Left Behind Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/No_Child_Left_Behind_Act). The Hawaii Board of Education requires all eligible students to take these tests and report all student test scores. This may have unbalanced the results that reported in August 2005 that of 282 schools across the state, 185 failed to reach federal minimum performance standards in mathematics and reading.[^cite_note-305] The [ACT college placement tests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ACT_\(examination\)) show that in 2005, seniors scored slightly above the national average (21.9 compared with 20.9),[^cite_note-306] but in the widely accepted [SAT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/SAT) examinations, Hawaii's college-bound seniors tend to score below the national average in all categories except mathematics.

The first native controlled public charter school was the [Kanu O Ka Aina New Century Charter School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kanu_O_Ka_Aina_New_Century_Charter_School).[^cite_note-307]

### Private schools

Hawaii has the highest rates of private school attendance in the nation. During the 2011–2012 school year, Hawaii public and charter schools had an enrollment of 181,213,[^cite_note-DOE-308] while private schools had 37,695.[^cite_note-HAIS-309] Private schools educated over 17% of students in Hawaii that school year, nearly three times the approximate national average of 6%.[^cite_note-NCES-310] According to Alia Wong of *[Honolulu Civil Beat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_Civil_Beat)*, this is due to private schools being relatively inexpensive compared to ones on the mainland as well as the overall reputations of private schools.[^cite_note-311]

It has four of the largest independent schools; [ʻIolani School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iolani_School), [Kamehameha Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamehameha_Schools), [Mid-Pacific Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mid-Pacific_Institute) and [Punahou School](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Punahou_School). [Pacific Buddhist Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pacific_Buddhist_Academy), the second Buddhist high school in the U.S. and first such school in Hawaii, was founded in 2003.

Independent schools can select their students, while most public schools of HIDOE are open to all students in their attendance zones. The Kamehameha Schools are the only schools in the U.S. that openly grant admission to students based on ancestry; collectively, they are one of the wealthiest schools in the United States, if not the world, having over eleven billion US dollars in estate assets.[^cite_note-312] In 2005, Kamehameha enrolled 5,398 students, 8.4% of the Native Hawaiian children in the state.[^cite_note-313]

### Colleges and universities

See also: [List of colleges and universities in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_colleges_and_universities_in_Hawaii)

A large stone or landmark made of a marble-like material reads "UNIVERSITY OF HAWAII AT MANOA", with the seal of the university at the top.

Main entrance of the [University of Hawaiʻi at Mānoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi_at_M%C4%81noa)

The largest institution of higher learning in Hawaii is the [University of Hawaiʻi System](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi_System), which consists of the research university at [Mānoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi_at_M%C4%81noa), two comprehensive campuses at [Hilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi_at_Hilo) and [West Oʻahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi_at_West_O%CA%BBahu), and seven community colleges. Private universities include [Brigham Young University–Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brigham_Young_University%E2%80%93Hawaii), [Chaminade University of Honolulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chaminade_University_of_Honolulu), [Hawaii Pacific University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Pacific_University), and [Wayland Baptist University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wayland_Baptist_University). [Saint Stephen Diocesan Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Stephen_Diocesan_Seminary,_Honolulu) is a [seminary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seminary) of the [Roman Catholic Diocese of Honolulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Diocese_of_Honolulu). Kona hosts the [University of the Nations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_the_Nations), which is not an [accredited](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Educational_accreditation) university.

## Transportation

Main article: [Transportation in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transportation_in_Hawaii)

See also: [Hawaii Department of Transportation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Department_of_Transportation), [List of airports in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_airports_in_Hawaii), and [Aviation in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aviation_in_Hawaii)



Daniel K. Inouye International Airport

A [system of state highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaii_state_highways) encircles each main island. Only Oʻahu has federal highways, and is the only area outside the contiguous 48 states to have signed [Interstate highways](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interstate_Highway_System). Narrow, winding roads and congestion in populated places can slow traffic. Each major island has a public bus system.

[Daniel K. Inouye International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_K._Inouye_International_Airport) ([IATA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Air_Transport_Association_airport_code): HNL), which shares runways with the adjacent [Hickam Field](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hickam_Field) (IATA: HIK), is the major commercial aviation hub of Hawaii. The airport was previously known as Honolulu International Airport, before its official renaming on May 30, 2017, after the late U.S. Senator [Daniel K. Inouye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Inouye).[^cite_note-314] The commercial aviation airport offers intercontinental service to North America, Asia, Australia and Oceania. [Hawaiian Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_Airlines) and [Mokulele Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mokulele_Airlines) use jets to provide services between the large airports in Honolulu, Līhuʻe, Kahului, Kona and Hilo. These airlines also provide air freight services between the islands.

Until air passenger services began in the 1920s,[^cite_note-Hawaii_Aviation-315] private boats were the sole means of traveling between the islands. Seaflite operated [hydrofoils](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hydrofoils) between the major islands in the mid-1970s.[^cite_note-316]

The [Hawaii Superferry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Superferry) operated between Oʻahu and Maui between December 2007 and March 2009, with additional routes planned for other islands. Protests and legal problems over environmental impact statements ended the service, though the company operating Superferry has expressed a wish to recommence ferry services in the future.[^cite_note-Alakai-317] Currently there is a passenger ferry service in [Maui County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maui_County) between Lanaʻi and Maui,[^cite_note-Lana'i_Ferry-318] which does not take vehicles; a passenger ferry to Molokaʻi ended in 2016.[^cite_note-319] Currently [Norwegian Cruise Lines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Norwegian_Cruise_Lines) and [Princess Cruises](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Princess_Cruises) provide passenger cruise ship services between the larger islands.[^cite_note-NCL-320][^cite_note-Princess_Cruises-321]

### Rail

At one time Hawaii had a network of railroads on each of the larger islands that transported farm commodities and passengers. Most were 3 ft (914 mm) [narrow gauge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Narrow_gauge) systems but there were some 2 ft 6 in (762 mm) gauge on some of the smaller islands. The standard gauge in the U.S. is 4 ft 8+1⁄2 in (1,435 mm). By far the largest railroad was the [Oahu Railway and Land Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oahu_Railway_and_Land_Company) (OR\&L) that ran lines from Honolulu across the western and northern part of Oʻahu.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-322]

The OR\&L was important for moving troops and goods during World War II. Traffic on this line was busy enough for signals to be used to facilitate movement of trains and to require [wigwag](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wigwag_\(railroad\)) signals at some railroad crossings for the protection of motorists. The main line was officially abandoned in 1947, although part of it was bought by the U.S. Navy and operated until 1970. Thirteen miles (21 km) of track remain; preservationists occasionally run trains over a portion of this line.[^cite_note-ReferenceA-322]

[Skyline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Skyline_\(Honolulu\)) is an elevated passenger rail line operated by [HART](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_Authority_for_Rapid_Transportation), a semi-autonomous agency of the [City and County of Honolulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_County,_Hawaii). It was built with the intention to relieve highway congestion.[^cite_note-323] A portion of Skyline opened for service in 2023, with the next phase expected to open in October 2025, and the final phase in 2031.[^cite_note-324]

## Governance

### Political subdivisions and local government

See also: [List of counties in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_counties_in_Hawaii)



The [Governor of Hawaii](253068.md) officially resides at [Washington Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Place), an old American-built residence

The movement of the Hawaiian royal family from Hawaiʻi Island to Maui, and subsequently to Oʻahu, explains the modern-day distribution of population centers. [Kamehameha III](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamehameha_III) chose the largest city, Honolulu, as his capital because of its natural harbor—the present-day [Honolulu Harbor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_Harbor). Now the state capital, Honolulu is located along the southeast coast of Oʻahu. The previous capital was [Lahaina](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lahaina,_Hawaii), Maui, and before that [Kailua-Kona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kailua-Kona), Hawaiʻi. Some major towns are [Hilo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hilo,_Hawaii); [Kaneohe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaneohe,_Hawaii); [Kailua](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kailua,_Honolulu_County,_Hawaii); [Pearl City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pearl_City,_Hawaii); [Waipahu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waipahu,_Hawaii); [Kahului](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kahului,_Hawaii); [Kailua-Kona](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kailua,_Hawaii_County,_Hawaii). [Kīhei](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kihei,_Hawaii); and [Līhuʻe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lihue,_Hawaii).

Hawaii has five counties: the [City and County of Honolulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_County,_Hawaii), [Hawaiʻi County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawai%CA%BBi_County,_Hawaii), [Maui County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maui_County,_Hawaii), [Kauaʻi County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaua%CA%BBi_County,_Hawaii), and [Kalawao County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalawao_County,_Hawaii).

Hawaii has the fewest local governments among U.S. states.[^cite_note-USCensus2010-325][^cite_note-USCensus2007-326] Unique to this state is the lack of [municipal governments](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Municipal_corporation). All local governments are generally administered at the [county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/County_\(United_States\)) level. The only incorporated area in the state is [Honolulu County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_County,_Hawaii), a [consolidated city–county](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consolidated_city%E2%80%93county) that governs the entire island of Oʻahu. County executives are referred to as mayors; these are the [Mayor of Hawaiʻi County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Hawai%CA%BBi_County), [Mayor of Honolulu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Honolulu), [Mayor of Kauaʻi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Kaua%CA%BBi), and the [Mayor of Maui](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mayor_of_Maui). The mayors are all elected in [nonpartisan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-partisan_democracy) elections. Kalawao County has no elected government,[^cite_note-327] and as [mentioned above](#Education) there are no local [school districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/School_district); instead, all local public education is administered at the state level by the [Hawaii Department of Education](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Department_of_Education). The remaining local governments are [special districts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special-purpose_district).[^cite_note-USCensus2010-325][^cite_note-USCensus2007-326]

### State government

Further information: [Category\:State agencies of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Category\:State_agencies_of_Hawaii)



[Hawaii State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_State_Capitol) building

The state government of Hawaii is modeled after the federal government with adaptations originating from the kingdom era of Hawaiian history. As codified in the [Constitution of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constitution_of_Hawaii), there are three [branches of government](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Branches_of_government): executive, legislative, and judicial, although the [Office of Hawaiian Affairs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Office_of_Hawaiian_Affairs) is often referred to as the fourth branch of government as it does not fall under the other three branches. The executive branch is led by the [Governor of Hawaii](253068.md), who is assisted by the [Lieutenant Governor of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lieutenant_Governor_of_Hawaii), both of whom are elected on the same ticket. The governor is the only state public official elected statewide; all others are appointed by the governor. The lieutenant governor acts as the [Secretary of State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Secretary_of_State_of_Hawaii). The governor and lieutenant governor oversee twenty agencies and departments from offices in the [State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_State_Capitol). The official residence of the governor is [Washington Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Washington_Place).

The legislative branch consists of the [bicameral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bicameralism) [Hawaii State Legislature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_State_Legislature), which is composed of the 51-member [Hawaii House of Representatives](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_House_of_Representatives) led by the [Speaker of the House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Speaker_\(politics\)), and the 25-member [Hawaii Senate](798044.md) led by the [President of the Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/President_of_the_Senate). The Legislature meets at the [State Capitol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_State_Capitol). The unified judicial branch of Hawaii is the [Hawaii State Judiciary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_State_Judiciary). The [state's highest court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_supreme_court) is the [Supreme Court of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Supreme_Court_of_Hawaii), which uses [Aliʻiōlani Hale](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aliiolani_Hale) as its chambers.

### Federal government

- Congressional delegation for the [118th United States Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/118th_United_States_Congress)

- Senator Brian Schatz

  Senator [Brian Schatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Schatz)

- Senator Mazie Hirono

  Senator [Mazie Hirono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazie_Hirono)

- Representative Ed Case (HI-1)

  Representative [Ed Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Case) ([HI-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii's_1st_congressional_district))

- Representative Jill Tokuda (HI-2)

  Representative [Jill Tokuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jill_Tokuda) ([HI-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii's_2nd_congressional_district))

Hawaii is represented in the [United States Congress](31756.md) by two senators and two [representatives](19468510.md). As of 2023, all four seats are held by Democrats. Former representative [Ed Case](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ed_Case) was elected in 2018 to the [1st congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii's_1st_congressional_district). [Jill Tokuda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jill_Tokuda) represents the [2nd congressional district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii's_2nd_congressional_district), representing the rest of the state, which is largely rural and semi-rural.[^cite_note-Representative_Jill_Tokuda-328]

[Brian Schatz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brian_Schatz) is the senior United States senator from Hawaii. He was appointed to the office on December 26, 2012, by Governor [Neil Abercrombie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neil_Abercrombie), following the death of former senator [Daniel Inouye](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Inouye). Schatz then won the 2014 special election, and the 2016 and 2022 regular elections in Hawaii as Senator.

The state's junior senator is [Mazie Hirono](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mazie_Hirono), the former representative from the second congressional district. She won in the 2012, 2018, and 2024 elections for Senator in Hawaii, following the retirement of [Daniel Akaka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_Akaka). Hirono is the first female Asian American senator and the first Buddhist senator.

Hawaii incurred the biggest [seniority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seniority_in_the_United_States_Senate) shift between the [112th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/112th_United_States_Congress) and [113th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/113th_United_States_Congress) Congresses. The state went from a delegation consisting of senators who were first and twenty-first in seniority[^cite_note-329] to their respective replacements, relative newcomers Schatz and Hirono.[^cite_note-330]

Federal officials in Hawaii are based at the [Prince Kūhiō Federal Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prince_Kuhio_Federal_Building) near the [Aloha Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aloha_Tower) and Honolulu Harbor. The [Federal Bureau of Investigation](11127.md), [Internal Revenue Service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Internal_Revenue_Service) and the [Secret Service](58221.md) maintain their offices there; the building is also the site of the [federal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_federal_courts) [District Court for the District of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_District_Court_for_the_District_of_Hawaii) and the [United States Attorney for the District of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_Attorney_for_the_District_of_Hawaii).

### Politics

Main article: [Politics of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Politics_of_Hawaii)

See also: [Political party strength in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_party_strength_in_Hawaii) and [United States presidential elections in Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_presidential_elections_in_Hawaii)



Hawaii-born President [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama) signs the [Zadroga Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zadroga_Act) in [Kailua, Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kailua,_Hawaii)

Since gaining statehood and participating in its first election in [1960](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1960_United_States_presidential_election), Hawaii has supported Democrats in all but two presidential elections: [1972](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_United_States_presidential_election_in_Hawaii) and [1984](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1984_United_States_presidential_election_in_Hawaii), both of which were landslide reelection victories for Republicans [Richard Nixon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Richard_Nixon) and [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) respectively. In Hawaii's statehood tenure, only Minnesota has supported Republican candidates fewer times in presidential elections. The 2022 [Cook Partisan Voting Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cook_Partisan_Voting_Index) ranks Hawaii as the third-most heavily Democratic state in the nation.[^cite_note-331]

Hawaii has not elected a Republican to represent the state in the U.S. Senate since [Hiram Fong](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hiram_Fong) in 1970; since 1977, both of the state's U.S. Senators have been Democrats.[^cite_note-332][^cite_note-333]

In [2004](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2004_United_States_presidential_election), [John Kerry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_Kerry) won the state's four electoral votes by a margin of nine percentage points with 54% of the vote. Every county supported the Democratic candidate. In 1964, [favorite son](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Favorite_son) candidate senator Hiram Fong of Hawaii sought the [Republican](32070.md) presidential nomination, while [Patsy Mink](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Patsy_Mink) ran in the Oregon primary in 1972.



Governor [David Ige](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/David_Ige) with U.S. Navy admiral [John Richardson](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/John_M._Richardson_\(admiral\)) at the 75th Commemoration Event of the Pearl Harbor and Oʻahu attacks in 2016

Honolulu-born [Barack Obama](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Barack_Obama), then serving as a [United States senator](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_States_senator) from [Illinois](14849.md), was elected the [44th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_presidents_of_the_United_States) president of the United States on [November 4, 2008](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2008_United_States_presidential_election), and was re-elected for a second term on [November 6, 2012](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2012_United_States_presidential_election). Obama had won the Hawaii Democratic caucus on February 19, 2008, with 76% of the vote. He was the third Hawaii-born candidate to seek the nomination of a major party, the first presidential nominee and first president from Hawaii.[^cite_note-334][^cite_note-335]

In a 2020 study, Hawaii was ranked as the 6th easiest state for citizens to vote in.[^cite_note-336]

### Law enforcement

Hawaii has a statewide sheriff department under its [Department of Public Safety](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaii_Department_of_Public_Safety) that provides law enforcement protection to government buildings and [Daniel K. Inouye International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Daniel_K._Inouye_International_Airport) as well as correction services to all correctional facilities owned by the state.

Counties have their own respective police departments with their own jurisdictions:

- [Kauaʻi County Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaua%CA%BBi_County_Police_Department) for the island of Kauaʻi

- [Honolulu Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_Police_Department) for Oʻahu

- [Maui County Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maui_County_Police_Department) for Molokaʻi, Maui and Lānaʻi

- [Hawaiʻi County Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawai%CA%BBi_County_Police_Department) for the Big Island

Forensic services for all agencies in the state are provided by the [Honolulu Police Department](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_Police_Department).[^cite_note-337]

In January 2022, state officials proposed legislation that would split the sheriff department from the Department of Public Safety and consolidate it with the criminal investigation division from the [Department of the Attorney General](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/State_of_Hawaii_Department_of_the_Attorney_General) to create a new Department of Law Enforcement that would create a statewide police agency with the ability to investigate crimes.[^cite_note-338]

## Hawaiian sovereignty movement

Main articles: [Hawaiian sovereignty movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_sovereignty_movement), [List of Hawaiian sovereignty movement groups](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Hawaiian_sovereignty_movement_groups), and [Legal status of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_status_of_Hawaii)



[Kaniakapupu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaniakapupu) royal summer palace ruins in [Honolulu County](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Honolulu_County)

While Hawaii is internationally recognized as a state of the United States while also being broadly accepted as such in mainstream understanding, the [legality of this status](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_status_of_Hawaii) has been questioned in U.S. District Court,[^cite_note-district-339] the U.N., and other international forums.[^cite_note-UN-forum-340] Domestically, the debate is a topic covered in the [Kamehameha Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kamehameha_Schools) curriculum,[^cite_note-curriculum-341] and in classes at the [University of Hawaiʻi at Mānoa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_of_Hawai%CA%BBi_at_M%C4%81noa).[^cite_note-342]

Political organizations seeking some form of sovereignty for Hawaii have been active since the late 19th century. Generally, their focus is on [self-determination](29269.md) and [self-governance](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Self-governance), either for Hawaii as an independent nation (in many proposals, for "Hawaiian nationals" descended from subjects of the Hawaiian Kingdom or declaring themselves as such by choice), or for people of whole or part [native Hawaiian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiian) ancestry in an indigenous "*nation to nation*" relationship akin to [tribal sovereignty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribal_sovereignty) with [US federal recognition of Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/US_federal_recognition_of_Native_Hawaiians). The pro-federal recognition [Akaka Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Akaka_Bill) drew substantial opposition among Hawaiian residents in the 2000s.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-HIunpo-344] Opponents to the tribal approach argue it is not a legitimate path to Hawaiian nationhood; they also argue that the U.S. government should not be involved in re-establishing Hawaiian sovereignty.[^cite_note-345][^cite_note-Perkins-346]

The [Hawaiian sovereignty movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hawaiian_sovereignty_movement) views the [overthrow of the Kingdom of Hawaii](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii) in 1893 as illegal, and views the subsequent [annexation of Hawaii by the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Newlands_Resolution) as illegal as well; the movement seeks some form of greater autonomy for Hawaii, such as [free association](346414.md) or independence from the United States.[^cite_note-HIunpo-344][^cite_note-Hawaii1893-347][^cite_note-Hawaiisov-348][^cite_note-349]

Some groups also advocate some form of redress from the United States for the [1893 overthrow](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Overthrow_of_the_Kingdom_of_Hawaii) of [Queen Liliʻuokalani](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Liliuokalani), and for what is described as a prolonged [military occupation](816214.md) beginning with the 1898 annexation. The [Apology Resolution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apology_Resolution) passed by US Congress in 1993 is cited as a major impetus by the movement for Hawaiian sovereignty.[^cite_note-Hawaii1893-347] The sovereignty movement considers Hawaii to be an illegally occupied nation.[^cite_note-Hawaiisov-348][^cite_note-350][^cite_note-Perkins-346]

[^cite_note-12]: &#x20;Local usage generally reserves *Hawaiian* as an [ethnonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethnonym) referring to [Native Hawaiians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Native_Hawaiians). *Hawaii resident* or *from Hawaii* is the preferred local form to refer to state residents in general regardless of ethnicity. *Hawaii* may also be used adjectivally. The *[AP Stylebook](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/AP_Stylebook)*, 42nd ed. (2007), also prescribes this usage (page 112).
